===== Module 6: Medications with Selective Toxicity [_7758066_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758066_1?view=content&state=view
Remember how our medications normally use our own cellular receptors or our own enzymes? Well our medications that combat infections or cancer will need to take a different approach. Let's find out how they do that.
Children:
Module overview
Shortened Chapter: Only the 43 minutes of compulsory lessons on Medications with Selective Toxicity
Medications with Selective Toxicity (Full chapter, including non-compulsory lessons and activities)
Polypharmacy 1: A reflective section

Introduction to Module 6
This module has only 43 minutes of compulsory lessons.
You may choose to engage in just the compulsory activities (choose the first chapter below) or choose the entire chapter which includes examples of specific antibiotics, antifungal agents, antiviral agents and chemotherapeutic agents by choosing the second chapter below. The third chapter is an interesting artistic reflection of the unit which is not compulsory.

[6.60] Hi there and welcome to our module. That's going to give you a really good picture of the medications exhibiting as selective toxicity.
[14.40] As we know, most of our medications work on our own cellular receptors.
[19.44] Or they work by inhibiting an enzyme that one of our normal cells produces.
[25.68] But we don't target our normal cells when we're using anti-infective agents.
[32.20] Instead, we administer a medication that exhibits a selective toxicity to that microbe,
[38.60] or to the group of cells that have started to proliferate out of control.
[43.08] For example, an antimicrobial agent must attack and disable some microbial process or structure that's characteristic of the microbe,
[53.20] but not of human cells. An example of that is the penicillins and the cephalosporins,
[59.72] and both of those act in a similar way to decrease the synthesis of the bacterial cell wall.
[67.16] And since humans don't have a bacterial cell wall, that's a great mechanism because it exhibits a selective toxicity to the microorganism.
[79.20] Another example of selective toxicity is an anti-cancer chemotherapeutic agent which will target the rapidly proliferating cells.
[90.21] Or in other words, the cells going through the cell cycle.
[94.45] So this portion of the module is dedicated toward the medications that exhibit a selective toxicity.
[100.85] In part A, we're going to get a really good feel for the background information for antibiotics.
[108.29] The information that you'll need to understand in order to understand the specific antibiotics in part B,
[114.93] we're going to take those specific antibiotics, but just about nine of them.
[119.17] And we'll learn just a good foundation for the antibiotics.
[124.41] In part C we're going to take a look at the antifungal agents.
[128.69] Really brief look on that one. Part D is going to be an investigation of the really important information on antiviral agents.
[139.85] And then we'll wind up our discussions of the agents that deliver a selective toxicity by talking about the chemotherapeutic anti-cancer agents.
[153.21] And we're going to have one other interesting topic in this module.
[156.81] So stay tuned. We'll see you there.

===== Shortened Chapter: Only the 43 minutes of compulsory lessons on Medications with Selective Toxicity [_7758115_1] =====

This portion of the module contains only the compulsory lessons (43 minutes of lessons). This does not contain information about specific medications but rather general information that is absolutely essential for you to know about bacteria, fungi, viruses, and cancerous cells, and the medications that exert selective toxicity towards them.

E/5: Anticancer chemotherapy

MECHANISM OF ACTION OF ANTIMICROBIAL AGENTS 
The ‘ideal’ antimicrobial agent will, after entering a person’s body, move to the site of infection, destroy the pathogenic microbe (or at least facilitate its destruction), then be eliminated from the body without affecting the structure or function of human body cells. To achieve this aim, it must attack and disable some microbial process or structure not present in humans. This is known as the principle of selective toxicity. Indeed, there are many differences between human cells and microbes to exploit (see Figure 70.1). 
The mechanism of action of any antimicrobial agent can be classified according to the way in which it exploits these differences. The four categories by which antimicrobial agents work are described here. 
Inhibition of cell wall synthesis 
Most bacteria possess a cell wall to protect them from the osmotic influences of the external environment. As these microbes divide to produce progeny, new cell walls are synthesised. Certain antimicrobial agents interrupt the formation of the cell wall in these progeny, leaving them vulnerable to outside influences. As a result, the cell ruptures and the microbes perish. 
Penicillins, 
cephalosporins
, 
vancomycin
 and bacitracin 
are examples of medicines that work in this way. These are relatively nontoxic antimicrobial agents, as human cells do not possess a cell wall. 
Interference with metabolic processes 
These medicines are structurally similar to para-
aminobenzoic
 acid (PABA), a component of folic acid. The latter substance is an essential participant in nucleic acid synthesis. Without folic acid, the bacteria cannot produce the proteins necessary for growth. Antimicrobial agents with this action inhibit the synthesis of folic acid by competition with PABA or incorporate themselves in the structure of folic acid, thus interfering with the metabolic reactions in which it would normally participate. The selectivity of these medicines derives from the fact that many microbes must synthesise their own folic acid, whereas humans can use folic acid present in their diet. The 
sulfonamides
 and trimethoprim are examples of medicines with this mechanism of action (see Chapter 71). 
Inhibition of protein synthesis 
There are two locations at which such medicines can act—either at the site of protein synthesis (the ribosome) or within the nucleus by preventing the synthesis of nucleic acids (inhibiting either DNA replication or RNA synthesis), thereby blocking transcription. Proteins are essential to repair and growth. As new proteins need to be manufactured before cell division, inhibition of this process halts microbial population growth. Examples of medicines that act in this way are the 
tetracyclines
 (e.g. doxycycline), aminoglycosides (e.g. gentamicin) and macrolides (e.g. erythromycin). These medicines exploit structural differences between human and bacterial ribosomes or enzymes involved in nucleic acid synthesis. However, at higher doses they can be quite toxic to human cells, which also need to synthesise proteins. 
Disruption or destabilising the microbial cell membrane 
Antimicrobial agents that act through this mechanism affect membrane transport into and out of the microbe. This change in cellular permeability is incompatible with survival. 
Colistin
 is an example of such medicines. Many of the antifungal agents also act by this mechanism (see Chapter 78). These medicines are more toxic systemically than the previous group, as some human cells (e.g. erythrocytes) are particularly vulnerable to their action. 

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide2.xml
Let’s just take a very broad overview of different infective agents. There are several different
 kinds of infective agents. The most common of these include bacteria, fungi and viruses. These are ubiquitous or in other words they are found everywhere. Well, virtually everywhere with the possible exception of inside an autoclave after it has been sterilising utensils for 30 minutes. Bacteria and fungi live together in a symbiotic fashion. The proliferation of one strain of bacteria is often kept in check with the resident fungi, and visa versa, fungi are often kept in check on skin flora with the toxins from the resident bacteria. Outside a host, fungi can proliferate on degrading organic material, because they are natures way of degrading organics. 
In comparison, if this picture of the bacteria and fungi were shown about 1 million times, the virus pictured has been blown up another 1000
 
times. Viruses are not life forms in themselves, but rather a protein coat around some DNA or RNA. 
If you look at the role of each of these in nature, 
Bacteria
 are the unicellular organisms and cannot be seen with naked eye. There is no particular method of cell division, they simply divide simply by binary fission in which cell divides into two daughter cells. They do no have proper nucleus within the cell but the genetic material is attached to the cell membrane in an irregular form. They are found everywhere like top of the mountains, rivers, on land and in ice. Bacteria have the property of living in extreme weathers like extreme cold and extreme heat. They are able to live long because they become inactive for a long period of time.
Bacteria play an important role in the environment. Many dead materials are decomposed by bacteria. If there were no bacteria, the environment would have been polluted and full of harmful microorganisms. They degrade the dead organic matter and convert it into energy and nutrients. For example, they decompose trees and get their food from them in the form of nutrition. 
Organic carbon present in the environment in the form dead organism is able to eat up all the carbon dioxide from the atmosphere if there were no decomposers in the on earth. It can be imagined that if carbon dioxide gets reduced from the atmosphere, there would have been no photosynthesis in plants and as a result no food would have been produced by plants. 
Decomposers or bacteria help in cycling of minerals like carbon and 
sulfur
.
 They are also helpful in making, drugs, antibodies and hormones. 
The role of viruses
 is less clear, but there is no doubt that evolution is influenced by viruses. In the entire world, it is estimated that 100 million metric tonnes of bacteria die of viral infections every 60 seconds. Viruses are no doubt important in keeping populations of bacteria and fungi in check, but they are also important to the evolution of all living things because they inject their own genetic material into the host cells.
Viruses do not fit the normal description of life. 
7

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide7.xml
ANTIBACTERIAL AGENTS THAT ATTACK CELL WALL SYNTHESIS 
. This group includes the penicillins, an 
cephalosporins
, 
General mechanism of action 
These antibacterial agents are bactericidal and inhibit the formation of the rigid cell wall of dividing bacteria. The consequences are lethal to susceptible bacteria but relatively harmless for human cells because the latter do not have cell walls. This mechanism is highlighted in Figure 72.1. 
General clinical considerations 
The major problems associated with the use of these antibacterial agents 
are the development of resistance in previously susceptible bacteria and serious allergic reactions.
 Resistance is due to the ability of bacteria to produce and enzymes that break
 down the antibiotics or in some way make them inactive. 
Penicillin and cephalosporin allergies arise out of the fact that these substances are derived from nonhuman sources and can precipitate a strong immune response on entering our bodies. Unfortunately, this reaction sometimes takes the form of a life-threatening anaphylaxis (see Chapter 18). 
31

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide9.xml
ANTIBACTERIAL AGENTS THAT INHIBIT PROTEIN SYNTHESIS 
General mechanism of action 
Most of the antibacterial agents that act by inhibiting protein synthesis are derived from products secreted by Streptomyces moulds. 
The
Specifically
, the site of action is usually (but not always) one of the ribosomal subunits, where proteins are manufactured. 
 selective toxicity of these medicines against microbial cells arises out of 
differences between the structure of prokaryote (microbial) and eukaryote (mammalian) ribosomes. 
However, at higher doses these 
antibacterials
 inhibit mammalian ribosomal processes as well.
 Protein synthesis inhibitors tend to have bacteri
ostatic
 properties at standard therapeutic doses. AMINOGLYCOSIDES 
The prototype aminoglycoside was streptomycin— 
historically, the next major antibacterial identified after penicillins and the first isolated from a Streptomyces mould. 
Today, it is rarely used clinically in Australia and New Zealand. The clinically important aminoglycosides are 
framycetin
, gentamicin, tobramycin, neomycin, 
paromomycin
 and 
amikacin
. 
Paromomycin
 is only available through the Special Access Scheme in Australia. 
Mechanism of action 
Even though they act by inhibiting protein synthesis (affecting the smaller ribosomal subunit) and, therefore, microbial growth, their potency at therapeutic dose levels affords them bactericidal properties. 
The spectrum of activity of most of these medicines is broad, effective against a wide range of Gram-negative bacteria (
making them important in the treatment of serious Gram-negative sepsis), staphylococci and, to a lesser degree, streptococci. The aminoglycosides are often used when microbes are found to be resistant to cheaper and less toxic antibacterial agents. 
Common adverse effects 
The toxicity of the aminoglycosides is well noted. 
The three body sites seriously affected by these medicines and the toxic effects observed are 
the kidneys (nephrotoxicity), 
the 
eighth cranial nerve (ototoxicity) and the neuromuscular junction (neuromuscular paralysis). 
These effects can be minimised by measuring peak and trough serum drug levels to ensure that they conform to the desired range, as well as monitoring the person for altered renal and vestibular functions. The neuromuscular effects can be avoided through slow intravenous administration. 
Clinical considerations 
The absorption of aminoglycoside antibacterial agents from the gastrointestinal tract is negligible, so 
for systemic effectiveness these medicines must be administered 
parenterally
.
 The exception is neomycin, which can be administered orally for bowel sterilisation before surgery. Neomycin is also available in combination with other antibacterial agents and/or a corticosteroid for topical use. The use of 
amikacin
 is usually reserved for situations in which resistance to the other aminoglycosides is apparent. 
Paromomycin
 is administered after completing treatment with metronidazole or 
tinidazole
. Thus, it is not used alone for intestinal disease. 
Paromomycin
 is taken with or after meals to reduce the incidence of drug-related diarrhoea. 
Once-daily dosing of aminoglycosides is currently thought to be as effective as more frequent dosing, and is generally recommended for individuals with normal renal function. If treatment is likely to continue for less than 48 hours, drug concentration monitoring is not required in people with normal renal function. However, for treatment longer than 48 hours, drug concentration and creatinine clearance should be checked every three to five days in clinically stable people, and daily in people with some form of clinical instability, especially those individuals with impaired renal function. A baseline creatinine clearance is determined for all people before treatment.
 
 
A number of strategies could be implemented to prevent aminoglycoside toxicity. Dose frequency should be once daily and certainly no more than 12-hourly, except in specified conditions, such as infective endocarditis or osteomyelitis. Gentamicin can be administered with 
benzylpenicillin
 to treat serious 
enterococcal
 infections, such as endocarditis. Duration of therapy should be relatively short and generally not more than 7–10 days. Cochlear toxicity can be monitored using audiometry testing every one or two weeks during prolonged treatment. Ensuring adequate hydration can help to prevent nephrotoxicity. If the renal function deteriorates, the aminoglycoside dosage may need to be adjusted or an alternative antibacterial agents considered. 
TETRACYCLINES 
Mechanism of action 
Tetracyclines
 are broad-spectrum, bacteriostatic antibacterial agents. As with the aminoglycosides, the mechanism of action is directed at the smaller ribosomal subunit. 
Demeclocycline
 is the only natural member of the tetracycline group used clinically, as all the others (tetracycline, 
lymecycline
, 
tigecycline
, doxycycline and minocycline) are either semisynthetic or synthetic derivatives. 
Tigecycline
 is a derivative of minocycline and is referred to as a member of the 
glycylcycline
 antibacterial agents. All the 
tetracyclines
 have a similar spectrum of activity and are effective against the Plasmodium spp. that cause malaria (see Chapter 76), many Mycoplasma, Chlamydia, 
rickettsiae
 and spirochaetes, and some amoebae. In terms of Gram-negative and Gram-positive bacteria, many strains have become resistant to the action of the 
tetracyclines
 and, therefore, culture and sensitivity tests are recommended. Tetracycline itself is not marketed in Australia, but it is available through the Special Access Scheme, if needed. 
Demeclocycline
 may be available in Australia through the Special Access Scheme. Similarly, the injection form of doxycycline is only marketed through the Special Access Scheme. 
Lymecycline
 is only available in New Zealand. 
Common adverse effects 
The most commonly observed adverse effects associated with the 
tetracyclines
 are gastrointestinal disturbances (nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, anorexia, abdominal pain), hypersensitivity reactions (skin rashes, discolouration of the nails, photosensitivity) and superinfection. This last effect is usually associated with prolonged treatment with 
tetracyclines
, and can be avoided by administering the antifungal agent nystatin in combination with tetracycline. Gastric irritation, 
oesophagitis
 and, more rarely, oesophageal ulceration, have been reported during doxycycline therapy. 
Clinical considerations 
Many of the 
tetracyclines
 are poorly absorbed from the gastrointestinal tract after oral administration; however, the resultant blood levels are still therapeutically effective. One of the major problems associated with the 
tetracyclines
 is their ability to bind to free divalent and trivalent ions, especially calcium (Ca2+), in the gut and blood. In the gut, this property results in less of the medicine being absorbed. This lack of absorption is especially problematic when 
tetracyclines
 are administered with food or 
nonsystemic
 antacids containing salts of aluminium, calcium and magnesium. The only exceptions are doxycycline and minocycline, which are very lipid-soluble 
tetracyclines
 whose absorption is not impaired by the presence of food in the gut. Antacids and preparations containing iron will decrease the absorption of all 
tetracyclines
. If these preparations need to be taken, they should be administered more than two hours after giving a tetracycline. Thus, 
tetracyclines
 should generally be taken one hour before or two hours after meals. 
The effect of binding calcium ions in the blood is that less calcium is stored in bones and teeth. This lack of calcium storage is the main reason why 
tetracyclines
 are not recommended for children under 8 years old or for women in the latter half of pregnancy. Newly forming teeth become permanently discoloured and poorly enamelled. Indeed, in children who have discoloured teeth as a result of exposure to tetracycline, you should be able to see the borderline separating affected and normal growth. If this line is close to the gums, then the exposure was later in tooth development. If it is closer to the crown, then exposure was early in development. Bone growth may also be affected. 
To reduce the risk of gastric and oesophageal reactions, it is recommended that tetracycline tablets or capsules be administered with a full glass of water and that the person not lie down for two hours after taking the medicine. The latter recommendation minimises drug contact with the oesophageal region. Ingestion just before bedtime should, therefore, be avoided. 
As 
tetracyclines
 may cause photosensitivity, people are advised to wear protective clothing when outside and to use sunscreen. The specific agent known as tetracycline is poorly absorbed from the gastrointestinal tract and, therefore, should be given on an empty stomach and in high
 
 
doses to obtain an adequate response. Unfortunately, these actions tend to increase the gastric irritation produced by tetracycline. 
People should be informed that 
tigecycline
 therapy may make them dizzy. They need to tell their doctor if they develop abdominal pain. Due to possible hypersensitivity with 
tetracyclines
, 
tigecycline
 should be used cautiously in these people. 
Tigecycline
 may prolong the prothrombin time and activated partial 
thromboplastin
 time, and cause thrombocytopenia in people with bleeding disorders. During long-term therapy with 
demeclocycline
, renal function should be monitored, especially in individuals with heart failure or liver cirrhosis, due to its effect on electrolyte and fluid balance. 
MACROLIDES
Erythromycin, clarithromycin and 
roxithromycin
 are macrolide antibacterial agents. Azithromycin is closely related, both chemically and pharmacologically, to the macrolides and is grouped with them. 
Mechanism of action
The macrolides specifically affect the function of the larger microbial ribosomal subunit without reacting with the human ribosome. This accounts for their relatively low human toxicity.
The spectrum of activity of this group resembles that of penicillin G; however, culture and sensitivity tests are recommended because resistance to previously sensitive strains has been demonstrated. Compared with erythromycin, the other medicines are less active against Gram-positive bacteria and more effective against Gram-negative bacteria, especially H. 
influenzae
. Azithromycin is very effective in the treatment of toxoplasmosis and may be more effective against Legionella and Chlamydia. Azithromycin, clarithromycin and 
roxithromycin
 also have a lower potential for hepatotoxicity than orally administered erythromycin. Because of their spectrum of activity, the macrolides are an important alternative for people with an allergy to penicillins or other related antibacterial agents. 
Common adverse effects
Unpleasant gastrointestinal disturbances and superinfection are common adverse reactions. The frequency of these adverse reactions is reduced when another medicine from this group besides erythromycin is used. 
Clinical considerations
The macrolides can be administered either orally or 
parenterally
. The newer medicines are more acid-stable than erythromycin. Some forms of erythromycin must be 
enterically
 coated for oral administration due to its acid-
lability
. Erythromycin has a bitter taste, which is somewhat reduced by the enteric coating; however, paediatric forms (granules, dulcets and suspensions) are flavoured to make them more palatable.
Macrolides are useful alternatives to penicillins and 
cephalosporins
 for individuals who are allergic to these medicines. Erythromycin and 
roxithromycin
 are best absorbed when taken about an hour before or two hours after meals. However, if nausea occurs they may be taken with food.
Administration of erythromycin or clarithromycin may cause prolongation of the Q-T interval and development of serious ventricular dysrhythmias. Rapid intravenous administration of erythromycin can cause the development of serious dysrhythmias, so it is important that the infusion rate not exceed 1–5 mg/mL over at least 60 minutes. Erythromycin and clarithromycin should not be administered with other medicines that have a tendency to prolong the Q-T interval, such as amitriptyline, haloperidol, 
amiodarone
 and 
pimozide
. Prolongation of the Q-T interval can lead to life-threatening cardiac rhythms, such as torsades de pointes.
It is also important to monitor for hepatic dysfunction during long-term or high-dose erythromycin therapy. Discontinue erythromycin therapy if severe hepatic dysfunction occurs, as demonstrated by changes in liver enzyme levels. 
STREPTOGRAMINS
Two members of the 
streptogramin
 antibacterial group, 
quinupristin
 and 
dalfopristin
, are available in Australia and New Zealand in a combined preparation. 
Mechanism of action
The 
streptogramins
 target protein synthesis at the ribosome. Each of these drugs induces a bacteriostatic action, but when used together the effect becomes a bactericidal action. They are indicated for intravenous administration in MDR S. Aureus or VRE infections when other antimicrobials are inappropriate. These medicines may also be useful in treating other 
multiresistant
 bacteria, such as Streptococcus and selected Gram-negative bacteria. 
Common adverse effects
Common adverse effects include reactions at the injection site, such as inflammation, pain and oedema. Systemic adverse reactions include nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea and rashes.
 
 
This combined preparation is administered intravenously every eight hours, and is prone to causing peripheral vein irritation. This effect can be reduced by using large infusion volumes of at least 1 L or if the combined preparation is infused through a central vein rather than through a peripheral vein. The preparation is not compatible with sodium chloride and should be administered with 5% glucose. Monitor the blood count periodically to determine whether thrombocytopenia or anaemia occurs. Arthralgia, myalgia and thrombophlebitis are common problems that can adversely affect people. Dosing every 12 hours or reducing the dose may help to resolve these problems. 
OXAZOLIDINONES 
Linezolid is the sole representative of the 
oxazolidinones
 available in this region. It, too, is recommended for use in MDR S. aureus and VRE infections. Linezolid is formulated for both oral and parenteral administration. 
Mechanism of action 
Linezolid acts to inhibit protein synthesis at the ribosome. However, its mechanism of action is different from that of other antibacterial agents. It binds to one of the subunits and prevents the two subunits from joining together. Subunit joining is essential for translation to occur. 
Common adverse effects 
Common adverse reactions include nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, headache, altered taste, abnormal liver function tests and superinfection with Candida 
albicans
. A reversible 
myelosuppression
 has also been observed, particularly when treatment is prolonged for more than 10–14 days. 
Clinical considerations 
Linezolid reversibly inhibits monoamine oxidase. People should avoid consuming foods that are rich in 
tyramine
 (e.g. mature cheese, yeast extracts) when taking linezolid as they may experience a rise in blood pressure. The mechanism by which 
tyramine
 and monoamine oxidase inhibition can interact to induce a hypertensive state is described in the chapter on antidepressant agents (see Chapter 36). Similarly, it should not be administered concurrently with monoamine oxidase inhibitor antidepressants. Due to its ability to increase synaptic concentration of serotonin, linezolid should not be administered with pethidine due to the potential for serotonin toxicity to occur. 
Long-term treatment of more than 21 days should be avoided to prevent the development of linezolid-resistant enterococci. This medicine is considered only if other 
antibacterials
 have failed, or they are inappropriate due to allergy or resistance. If linezolid treatment continues beyond 14 days, people should have their complete blood counts monitored each week. They should also be monitored for optic or peripheral neuropathy, especially if treatment is to be continued for more than 28 days. 
Other 
inactivators
 of the larger ribosomal subunit 
The 
lincosamides
, chloramphenicol and 
fusidic
 acid inhibit protein synthesis by inactivating the larger ribosomal unit. The 
lincosamides
 and chloramphenicol are all naturally occurring 
antibacterials
 derived from Streptomyces. 
Fusidic
 acid, derived from a species of 
Fusidium
 mould, is chemically related to the 
cephalosporins
. 
LINCOSAMIDES 
The 
lincosamides
 
lincomycin
 and clindamycin are both narrow-spectrum antibacterial agents not unlike erythromycin—effective against Gram-positive but not Gram-negative bacteria. Clindamycin is the more potent medicine and is better absorbed from the gastrointestinal tract. 
Common adverse effects 
The most common adverse reactions are gastrointestinal disturbances, such as antibiotic-associated colitis, hypersensitivity reactions and blood 
dyscrasias
. 
Clinical considerations 
Treatment with a 
lincosamide
 should stop immediately if significant diarrhoea or antibiotic-associated colitis occurs. A faecal specimen should be taken to identify the toxin. Metronidazole is the first-line treatment if antibiotic-associated colitis occurs. Undertake a complete blood examination, and assess the hepatic and renal functions during prolonged treatment with 
lincomycin
 or clindamycin. 
CHLORAMPHENICOL 
Chloramphenicol has a broader spectrum of activity and is effective against both Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria, 
rickettsiae
, Chlamydia and 
Coxiella
. 
Common adverse effects 
It is particularly toxic to mammalian cells, especially bone marrow cells, and as a result can induce aplastic anaemia. Its systemic use is restricted to the treatment of severe infections unresponsive to other antimicrobials
 
 
 
(e.g. typhoid fever, bacterial meningitis and 
rickettsial
 infections). Toxic side-effects are minimised when chloramphenicol is used topically as eye/ear drops or ointment (see Chapter 83). 
Clinical considerations
In Australia, the injection, capsule and oral liquid formulations of chloramphenicol are not marketed in Australia, but they can be obtained through the Special Access Scheme. When using systemic chloramphenicol, monitor the full blood count, platelets, serum iron and reticulocyte levels before and during therapy. Systemic use of chloramphenicol is limited by its severe toxicity. It is not used prophylactically or for minor infections. Specific indications relate to its use, such as the treatment of severe typhoid or paratyphoid fever if quinolones or 
cephalosporins
 are not suitable, and the treatment of meningitis, brain abscess or acute epiglottitis in people allergic to penicillins or 
cephalosporins
. Stop the medicine immediately if anaemia, 
granulocytopenia
 or thrombocytopenia develops. Advise the person to report if a sore throat, nausea, vomiting, fever or mouth sores develop during therapy. 
FUSIDIC ACID
Although it is chemically related to the 
cephalosporins
, the mechanism of action of 
fusidic
 acid is not prevention of cell wall synthesis, but inhibition of protein synthesis. It is a narrow-spectrum drug effective against Gram-positive bacteria; however, its use is restricted to the treatment of MDR S. aureus. Clinically, it is available as the sodium salt (sodium 
fusidate
), which is better absorbed from the gut. 
Common adverse effects
Common side-effects observed after 
fusidic
 acid treatment are allergic reactions and mild gastrointestinal disturbances. 
Clinical considerations
With 
fusidic
 acid, resistance to MDR S. aureus develops rapidly if it is used alone. It is best used in its salt form, sodium 
fusidate
, in combination with another antimicrobial agent, such as rifampicin. The use of a combination reduces the risk of resistance. Liver function should be monitored when using high doses of 
fusidic
 acid or during prolonged treatment. The tablet formulation is best absorbed on an empty stomach and tablets are swallowed whole. The injectable form of 
fusidic
 acid is not available in Australia; however, it can be obtained through the Special Access Scheme if required.

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide11.xml
ANTIBACTERIAL AGENTS THAT INHIBIT PROTEIN SYNTHESIS 
General mechanism of action 
Most of the antibacterial agents that act by inhibiting protein synthesis are derived from products secreted by Streptomyces moulds. 
The
Specifically
, the site of action is usually (but not always) one of the ribosomal subunits, where proteins are manufactured. 
 selective toxicity of these medicines against microbial cells arises out of 
differences between the structure of prokaryote (microbial) and eukaryote (mammalian) ribosomes. 
However, at higher doses these 
antibacterials
 inhibit mammalian ribosomal processes as well.
 Protein synthesis inhibitors tend to have bacteri
ostatic
 properties at standard therapeutic doses. AMINOGLYCOSIDES 
The prototype aminoglycoside was streptomycin— 
historically, the next major antibacterial identified after penicillins and the first isolated from a Streptomyces mould. 
Today, it is rarely used clinically in Australia and New Zealand. The clinically important aminoglycosides are 
framycetin
, gentamicin, tobramycin, neomycin, 
paromomycin
 and 
amikacin
. 
Paromomycin
 is only available through the Special Access Scheme in Australia. 
Mechanism of action 
Even though they act by inhibiting protein synthesis (affecting the smaller ribosomal subunit) and, therefore, microbial growth, their potency at therapeutic dose levels affords them bactericidal properties. 
The spectrum of activity of most of these medicines is broad, effective against a wide range of Gram-negative bacteria (
making them important in the treatment of serious Gram-negative sepsis), staphylococci and, to a lesser degree, streptococci. The aminoglycosides are often used when microbes are found to be resistant to cheaper and less toxic antibacterial agents. 
Common adverse effects 
The toxicity of the aminoglycosides is well noted. 
The three body sites seriously affected by these medicines and the toxic effects observed are 
the kidneys (nephrotoxicity), 
the 
eighth cranial nerve (ototoxicity) and the neuromuscular junction (neuromuscular paralysis). 
These effects can be minimised by measuring peak and trough serum drug levels to ensure that they conform to the desired range, as well as monitoring the person for altered renal and vestibular functions. The neuromuscular effects can be avoided through slow intravenous administration. 
Clinical considerations 
The absorption of aminoglycoside antibacterial agents from the gastrointestinal tract is negligible, so 
for systemic effectiveness these medicines must be administered 
parenterally
.
 The exception is neomycin, which can be administered orally for bowel sterilisation before surgery. Neomycin is also available in combination with other antibacterial agents and/or a corticosteroid for topical use. The use of 
amikacin
 is usually reserved for situations in which resistance to the other aminoglycosides is apparent. 
Paromomycin
 is administered after completing treatment with metronidazole or 
tinidazole
. Thus, it is not used alone for intestinal disease. 
Paromomycin
 is taken with or after meals to reduce the incidence of drug-related diarrhoea. 
Once-daily dosing of aminoglycosides is currently thought to be as effective as more frequent dosing, and is generally recommended for individuals with normal renal function. If treatment is likely to continue for less than 48 hours, drug concentration monitoring is not required in people with normal renal function. However, for treatment longer than 48 hours, drug concentration and creatinine clearance should be checked every three to five days in clinically stable people, and daily in people with some form of clinical instability, especially those individuals with impaired renal function. A baseline creatinine clearance is determined for all people before treatment.
 
 
A number of strategies could be implemented to prevent aminoglycoside toxicity. Dose frequency should be once daily and certainly no more than 12-hourly, except in specified conditions, such as infective endocarditis or osteomyelitis. Gentamicin can be administered with 
benzylpenicillin
 to treat serious 
enterococcal
 infections, such as endocarditis. Duration of therapy should be relatively short and generally not more than 7–10 days. Cochlear toxicity can be monitored using audiometry testing every one or two weeks during prolonged treatment. Ensuring adequate hydration can help to prevent nephrotoxicity. If the renal function deteriorates, the aminoglycoside dosage may need to be adjusted or an alternative antibacterial agents considered. 
TETRACYCLINES 
Mechanism of action 
Tetracyclines
 are broad-spectrum, bacteriostatic antibacterial agents. As with the aminoglycosides, the mechanism of action is directed at the smaller ribosomal subunit. 
Demeclocycline
 is the only natural member of the tetracycline group used clinically, as all the others (tetracycline, 
lymecycline
, 
tigecycline
, doxycycline and minocycline) are either semisynthetic or synthetic derivatives. 
Tigecycline
 is a derivative of minocycline and is referred to as a member of the 
glycylcycline
 antibacterial agents. All the 
tetracyclines
 have a similar spectrum of activity and are effective against the Plasmodium spp. that cause malaria (see Chapter 76), many Mycoplasma, Chlamydia, 
rickettsiae
 and spirochaetes, and some amoebae. In terms of Gram-negative and Gram-positive bacteria, many strains have become resistant to the action of the 
tetracyclines
 and, therefore, culture and sensitivity tests are recommended. Tetracycline itself is not marketed in Australia, but it is available through the Special Access Scheme, if needed. 
Demeclocycline
 may be available in Australia through the Special Access Scheme. Similarly, the injection form of doxycycline is only marketed through the Special Access Scheme. 
Lymecycline
 is only available in New Zealand. 
Common adverse effects 
The most commonly observed adverse effects associated with the 
tetracyclines
 are gastrointestinal disturbances (nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, anorexia, abdominal pain), hypersensitivity reactions (skin rashes, discolouration of the nails, photosensitivity) and superinfection. This last effect is usually associated with prolonged treatment with 
tetracyclines
, and can be avoided by administering the antifungal agent nystatin in combination with tetracycline. Gastric irritation, 
oesophagitis
 and, more rarely, oesophageal ulceration, have been reported during doxycycline therapy. 
Clinical considerations 
Many of the 
tetracyclines
 are poorly absorbed from the gastrointestinal tract after oral administration; however, the resultant blood levels are still therapeutically effective. One of the major problems associated with the 
tetracyclines
 is their ability to bind to free divalent and trivalent ions, especially calcium (Ca2+), in the gut and blood. In the gut, this property results in less of the medicine being absorbed. This lack of absorption is especially problematic when 
tetracyclines
 are administered with food or 
nonsystemic
 antacids containing salts of aluminium, calcium and magnesium. The only exceptions are doxycycline and minocycline, which are very lipid-soluble 
tetracyclines
 whose absorption is not impaired by the presence of food in the gut. Antacids and preparations containing iron will decrease the absorption of all 
tetracyclines
. If these preparations need to be taken, they should be administered more than two hours after giving a tetracycline. Thus, 
tetracyclines
 should generally be taken one hour before or two hours after meals. 
The effect of binding calcium ions in the blood is that less calcium is stored in bones and teeth. This lack of calcium storage is the main reason why 
tetracyclines
 are not recommended for children under 8 years old or for women in the latter half of pregnancy. Newly forming teeth become permanently discoloured and poorly enamelled. Indeed, in children who have discoloured teeth as a result of exposure to tetracycline, you should be able to see the borderline separating affected and normal growth. If this line is close to the gums, then the exposure was later in tooth development. If it is closer to the crown, then exposure was early in development. Bone growth may also be affected. 
To reduce the risk of gastric and oesophageal reactions, it is recommended that tetracycline tablets or capsules be administered with a full glass of water and that the person not lie down for two hours after taking the medicine. The latter recommendation minimises drug contact with the oesophageal region. Ingestion just before bedtime should, therefore, be avoided. 
As 
tetracyclines
 may cause photosensitivity, people are advised to wear protective clothing when outside and to use sunscreen. The specific agent known as tetracycline is poorly absorbed from the gastrointestinal tract and, therefore, should be given on an empty stomach and in high
 
 
doses to obtain an adequate response. Unfortunately, these actions tend to increase the gastric irritation produced by tetracycline. 
People should be informed that 
tigecycline
 therapy may make them dizzy. They need to tell their doctor if they develop abdominal pain. Due to possible hypersensitivity with 
tetracyclines
, 
tigecycline
 should be used cautiously in these people. 
Tigecycline
 may prolong the prothrombin time and activated partial 
thromboplastin
 time, and cause thrombocytopenia in people with bleeding disorders. During long-term therapy with 
demeclocycline
, renal function should be monitored, especially in individuals with heart failure or liver cirrhosis, due to its effect on electrolyte and fluid balance.

MECHANISM OF ACTION OF ANTIMICROBIAL AGENTS 
The ‘ideal’ antimicrobial agent will, after entering a person’s body, move to the site of infection, destroy the pathogenic microbe (or at least facilitate its destruction), then be eliminated from the body without affecting the structure or function of human body cells. To achieve this aim, it must attack and disable some microbial process or structure not present in humans. This is known as the principle of selective toxicity. Indeed, there are many differences between human cells and microbes to exploit (see Figure 70.1). 
The mechanism of action of any antimicrobial agent can be classified according to the way in which it exploits these differences. The four categories by which antimicrobial agents work are described here. 
Inhibition of cell wall synthesis 
Most bacteria possess a cell wall to protect them from the osmotic influences of the external environment. As these microbes divide to produce progeny, new cell walls are synthesised. Certain antimicrobial agents interrupt the formation of the cell wall in these progeny, leaving them vulnerable to outside influences. As a result, the cell ruptures and the microbes perish. 
Penicillins, 
cephalosporins
, 
vancomycin
 and bacitracin 
are examples of medicines that work in this way. These are relatively nontoxic antimicrobial agents, as human cells do not possess a cell wall. 
Interference with metabolic processes 
These medicines are structurally similar to para-
aminobenzoic
 acid (PABA), a component of folic acid. The latter substance is an essential participant in nucleic acid synthesis. Without folic acid, the bacteria cannot produce the proteins necessary for growth. Antimicrobial agents with this action inhibit the synthesis of folic acid by competition with PABA or incorporate themselves in the structure of folic acid, thus interfering with the metabolic reactions in which it would normally participate. The selectivity of these medicines derives from the fact that many microbes must synthesise their own folic acid, whereas humans can use folic acid present in their diet. The 
sulfonamides
 and trimethoprim are examples of medicines with this mechanism of action (see Chapter 71). 
Inhibition of protein synthesis 
There are two locations at which such medicines can act—either at the site of protein synthesis (the ribosome) or within the nucleus by preventing the synthesis of nucleic acids (inhibiting either DNA replication or RNA synthesis), thereby blocking transcription. Proteins are essential to repair and growth. As new proteins need to be manufactured before cell division, inhibition of this process halts microbial population growth. Examples of medicines that act in this way are the 
tetracyclines
 (e.g. doxycycline), aminoglycosides (e.g. gentamicin) and macrolides (e.g. erythromycin). These medicines exploit structural differences between human and bacterial ribosomes or enzymes involved in nucleic acid synthesis. However, at higher doses they can be quite toxic to human cells, which also need to synthesise proteins. 
Disruption or destabilising the microbial cell membrane 
Antimicrobial agents that act through this mechanism affect membrane transport into and out of the microbe. This change in cellular permeability is incompatible with survival. 
Colistin
 is an example of such medicines. Many of the antifungal agents also act by this mechanism (see Chapter 78). These medicines are more toxic systemically than the previous group, as some human cells (e.g. erythrocytes) are particularly vulnerable to their action. 

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide13.xml
Fungi
 are difficult to control because of the fact that their structure is similar to human cells and they have a phenotypic switching
Many pathogenic fungi exhibit a phenomenon known as 
dimorphism
, in which two different types of growth forms exist. The most common type of growth and the one with which most people are familiar is the MOULD (
mycelial
 or 
hyphal
 growth). 
The other type of growth occurs when the fungal cells exist LIKE A YEAST. 
Depending on environmental conditions, a fungus can exist in either of these forms. 
This is sometimes of clinical importance because in infected tissues the form is usually yeast, but when grown in culture the form is a typical mould
Filamentous form - A fine or thinly spun thread, 
fiber
, or wire.
53

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide14.xml
Fungi
 are difficult to control because of the fact that their structure is similar to human cells and they have a phenotypic switching

, in which two different types of growth forms exist. The most common type of growth and the one with which most people are familiar is the MOULD (

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide17.xml
Miscellaneous agents
CICLOPIROX 
Ciclopirox
 
is used in the treatment of 
superficial mycoses. 
Mechanism of action 
Ciclopirox
 is a synthetic 
antimycotic
 that is chemically classed as a 
hydroxypyridone
. Its mechanism of action is poorly understood. There is evidence that it chelates polyvalent metal ions, such as iron and aluminium. Enzyme systems dependent on these ions are inhibited, especially those enzymes involved in the removal of peroxides from fungal cells. It has a broad spectrum of activity. In some fungal species it is fungicidal, whereas in others it is 
fungistatic
. 
Ciclopirox
 also has anti-inflammatory activity, which is thought to be associated with an inhibition of the formation of chemical mediators, such as leukotrienes and prostaglandins. 
Common adverse effects 
As a topical medicine, 
ciclopirox
 is well tolerated. Common adverse reactions include itchiness and a burning sensation at the site of administration. 
Clinical considerations 
Ciclopirox
 is available as a shampoo that is applied to the hair for 
seborrhoeic
 dermatitis. It is used two to three times each week. The person should be forewarned that on rare occasions, 
ciclopirox
 may change the colour of white hair or 
artifically
 coloured hair
59

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide18.xml
Polyene
 antifungal agents 
Amphotericin 
and 
nystatin 
(the latter of which, interestingly, derives its name from the state of New York) belong to a class of antibiotics known as the 
polyenes
 because of the high number of double bonds in their structure. 
Nystatin is much more toxic when given systemically, so it is reserved for topical use only. 
Mechanism of action 
The 
polyenes
 bind to 
ergosterol
 and disrupt fungal cell membranes, upsetting normal membrane permeability. Thus, they cause death of the fungus when given in large doses. Smaller doses are 
fungistatic
. The 
polyenes
 can also bind to cholesterol but not quite as strongly, so there is usually less harm done to the host’s cell membranes. 
Common adverse effects 
Amphotericin is toxic, but in life-threatening situations large doses may have to be given. Although amphotericin has many adverse effects, it is often the only alternative available in potentially fatal fungal infections. A large dose will invariably cause severe renal impairment, which may take some months to return to normal. Other adverse effects include anorexia, malaise and fever. It should be noted that people with pre-existing cardiac or respiratory disease have a poor tolerance for febrile reactions associated with amphotericin. 
Intrathecal
 use may result in central nervous system (CNS) disturbances, such as 
paraesthesias
. When used topically, the only problem appears to be occasional sensitivity. 
Usually, no adverse effects are associated with nystatin when it is used topically. 
Clinical considerations 
Amphotericin is poorly absorbed from the gastrointestinal tract. 
For systemic infections, it must, therefore, be given 
parenterally
,
 although it can be used to treat oral and/or gastrointestinal candidiasis in the form of a lozenge. It is also available as a topical preparation for cutaneous and 
mucocutaneous
 infections. When used for CNS infections, it may have to be given 
intrathecally
. The oral lozenge form of amphotericin is better given after meals to alleviate problems associated with nausea, vomiting and diarrhoea. 
Amphotericin is available also in liposomal form for parenteral therapy, where the amphotericin molecule is 
complexed
 with phospholipid for more direct delivery of the drug to its site of action. The liposomal form of parenteral therapy is associated with a lower incidence of nephrotoxicity, fever, chills and nausea than the conventional therapy. Thus, higher doses of the liposomal form can be tolerated than the conventional form of parenteral therapy. Monitor serum creatinine, potassium, magnesium and blood urea levels, blood count and liver function during parenteral treatment. The high cost of the lipid complex limits its use to individuals with existing renal impairment or to those at risk of developing nephrotoxicity. 
Like amphotericin, nystatin is not absorbed when given orally. It is, thus, useful to treat intestinal candidiasis. It is available as 
pessaries
 and vaginal cream to treat fungal vaginitis. 
Resistance to nystatin does not generally occur. If taken orally, it can be useful as a prophylactic against candidiasis in people treated with broad-spectrum antibiotics for prolonged periods. It can also be used as a prophylactic for immunocompromised individuals, such as those with HIV. The nystatin liquid or lozenge is best used after a meal or drink rather than before a meal to avoid problems associated with nausea, vomiting and diarrhoea. The liquid is swished around the mouth for as long as possible before swallowing. Treatment with nystatin is continued for about two days after 
Candida 
symptoms have disappeared. 
61

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide19.xml
65

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide20.xml
66
Viruses do not fit the normal description of life. 
Perhaps it is better to think of viruses as infectious agents that enter cells and interfere with their normal function. A virus itself is not a cell, but a strand of either DNA or RNA, which has a protein coat and sometimes a lipid envelope. There is no cell membrane or cytoplasm. When a virus particle, a 
virion
, enters a cell, it uses the host cell enzymes to synthesise more of its nucleic acid and the components of its 
extranucleic
 structures. The viral nucleic acid can code for specific viral enzymes. A virus employs at least some of the existing metabolic machinery in the host cell for its own use. It is, therefore, very difficult to come up with a medicine that can destroy the virus without affecting the host cell. The process of infecting a host cell, viral synthesis and release of new viruses is represented in Figure 77.1. 
Most antiviral agents target the differences that exist between viral nucleic acids or enzymes, and those of the human host. Common sites of antiviral drug action are represented in Figure 77.2. 

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide21.xml
HIV INFECTIONS 
The human immunodeficiency virus (HIV), is a retrovirus and is caused mainly by two viral strains, HIV-1 and HIV-2. HIV-1 is found worldwide, whereas HIV-2 infections are commonest in parts of Africa and India. 
A 
retrovirus
 is a single-stranded 
RNA virus
 that stores its nucleic acid in the form of an 
mRNA
 genome 
Once inside the host cell 
cytoplasm
, the virus uses its own 
reverse transcriptase
 enzyme to produce 
DNA
 from its 
RNA
 genome, the reverse of the usual pattern, thus 
retro
 (backwards). 
This new DNA is then 
incorporated
 into the host cell 
genome
 by an 
integrase
 enzyme, at which point the retroviral DNA is referred to as a 
provirus
. The host cell then treats the viral DNA as part of its own 
genome
, translating and transcribing the viral genes along with the cell's own genes, producing the proteins required to assemble new copies of the virus. It is difficult to detect the virus until it has infected the host. At that point, the infection will persist indefinitely.
The antiviral agents used in the suppression of HIV are mostly effective against the HIV-1 strain
. 
The virus infects the CD4 cells (a group of helper T lymphocytes that possess the CD4 membrane receptor), thus decreasing the effectiveness of the cell-mediated immune systems. 
Two of the most  important drug groups used to suppress HIV are reverse transcriptase inhibitors and HIV protease inhibitors. The viral enzymes reverse transcriptase and protease are more susceptible to AIDS medicines than are human enzymes.
 Humans do not have the reverse transcriptase enzyme; however, these enzyme inhibitors can slightly affect other enzymes involved in human nucleic acid metabolism. 
A number of other medicines are also available for use in the management of HIV and AIDS, including 
fusion inhibitors, 
74

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide22.xml
DNA polymerase inhibitors
This group of antivirals includes 
aciclovir
, 
cidofovir
, 
entecavir
, 
famciclovir
, 
ganciclovir
, 
telbivudine
, 
valaciclovir
 
and 
valganciclovir
. The action of 
foscarnet
, at least in part, also places it in this grouping. 
Telbivudine
 is only available in Australia.
Mechanism of action
Aciclovir
 (sometimes spelt acyclovir) is a guanine derivative that acts as a 
prodrug
. It must be phosphorylated before it becomes active. It is this property that makes 
aciclovir
 such a useful antiviral agent in at least one viral infection—that is, the one caused by herpes virus type I. If a cell is infected with this virus, the viral enzyme thymidine kinase is present in the cell. This enzyme converts 
aciclovir
 into acyclovir 
monophosphate. Other enzymes, in turn, convert this into 
aciclovir
 triphosphate, which is the active form of the drug. 
It forms a complex with the enzyme DNA polymerase, which is needed for DNA replication, and inactivates it. This process occurs only in infected cells, and much higher concentrations of the drug are needed to affect host DNA polymerase. 
Aciclovir
, therefore, selectively harms only the virus in infected cells. 
As already stated, this medicine is best for the herpes type I virus. It does, however, have some deleterious effects on other viruses, such as varicella, herpes zoster (shingles) and Epstein–Barr virus. This medicine is not a cure for herpes but will merely suppress the symptoms and inhibit further replication of the virus. As with many viral infections, the virus can cause what is known as a latent or dormant infection, which can recur at a later date. It appears that the virus goes into ‘hiding’ away from the body’s immune system, and when circumstances permit it reappears to cause a clinical infection. 
Famciclovir
, like 
aciclovir
, is a 
prodrug
 and is converted into the active form, 
penciclovir
, within the body. The action of 
penciclovir
 is identical to that of 
aciclovir
 but has certain advantages in that it is better absorbed from the gut and has a longer half-life than the active form of 
aciclovir
. Compared to 
aciclovir
, less frequent dosing is required, resulting in better treatment adherence, especially during long-term therapy. 
Famciclovir
 has less activity on the type II virus than on the type I form. This relative lack of activity on the type II virus may lessen its usefulness in the treatment of genital herpes. 
Famciclovir
 is particularly useful in the treatment of shingles. Treatment must be given within 48 hours of appearance of the rash. 
Valaciclovir
 is a derivative of the amino acid 
valine
 and 
aciclovir
. (
Valaciclovir
 is, therefore, a pro-
prodrug
.) It has a very high bioavailability and a high hepatic first pass and is converted into 
aciclovir
. The advantage over 
aciclovir
 is that treatment with 
valaciclovir
 can attain blood concentrations of 
aciclovir
 equivalent to those attained when 
aciclovir
 is administered 
parenterally
. As 
aciclovir
 has probably fewer adverse effects and drug interactions, 
valaciclovir
 may prove to be one of the best of the antiviral agents for the treatment of herpes zoster, for the treatment and suppression of genital herpes, and for prophylaxis of CMV infection and disease in immunocompromised people. 
Ganciclovir, like 
aciclovir
, is a guanine derivative that can be used to treat CMV infections in immunocompromised individuals, such as people with AIDS. 
Valganciclovir
 is a 
valine
 derivative of ganciclovir and acts as a 
prodrug
. 
Cidofovir
 is a cytidine derivative, which is also used for CMV infections in immunocompromised patients. Medicines from this group have an inhibitory effect on DNA polymerase of CMV that is 600–800 times greater than that on the human enzyme. 
Telbividine
 is indicated in the treatment of chronic hepatitis B in nucleoside-naive people with compensated liver disease. It is a thymidine nucleoside analogue that competes with the natural thymidine nucleoside, thymidine triphosphate, and inhibits hepatitis B DNA polymerase. This action disrupts the replication of viral DNA. 
Entecavir
 is also indicated in the therapy of chronic hepatitis B in adults and children 16 years and older with active liver inflammation. It is a 
guanosine
 nucleoside analogue that is phosphorylated to 
entecavir
 triphosphate. It competes with the natural substrate, 
deoxyguanosine
 triphosphate, to inhibit the activity of hepatitis B DNA polymerase. 
Common adverse effects 
As might be expected from the mode of action, 
famciclovir
, 
valaciclovir
 and 
aciclovir
 have minimal adverse effects. Common adverse effects include headache, nausea and vomiting. 
Ganciclovir and 
cidofovir
 often cause 
neutropoenia
 and are potentially carcinogenic. Ganciclovir can induce aplastic anaemia. Hence, they are used only in life-threatening infections. 
Cidofovir
 is nephrotoxic and should not be used in people with renal impairment, or in combination with or after the use of nephrotoxic medicines. The concomitant use of 
probenecid
 
helps prevent nephrotoxic reactions. 
Clinical considerations 
In genital herpes, 
aciclovir
 speeds up the healing rate of the lesions and decreases the associated pain. It can be given orally, intravenously or applied as an ointment. Topical 
aciclovir
 is useful to treat herpes infections of the eyes and lips. However, topical 
aciclovir
 is considered less effective than the oral form of treatment. 
Aciclovir
 is administered intravenously to treat immunocompromised people as well as individuals with herpes simplex encephalitis. As oral 
aciclovir
 has a comparatively low bioavailability, parenteral administration is often needed in severe viral infections. Missed 
aciclovir
 tablets may produce blood levels at less than the minimum effective concentration. This can lead to ineffectiveness of the treatment. People need to be counselled about the need to comply with oral treatment to ensure maximum effectiveness. During 
aciclovir
 therapy, it is important to ensure that the person drinks adequate fluids (at least 2.5 L/day) to prevent renal impairment. Adequate hydration is particularly important for intravenous administration and when high doses are 
used. Tablets can be dispersed in water or swallowed whole. Advise people to start treatment as soon as possible if using 
aciclovir
 for shingles. Administration should start at least 48 hours after onset of the shingles rash; otherwise, little benefit is gained from treatment. Although early treatment of shingles reduces the duration of acute neuralgia, 
aciclovir
 treatment does not lessen the duration of 
postherpetic
 pain. Use cautiously in people with neurological disorders, renal disease or dehydration.
Cidofovir
 is given intravenously using veins with good flow. It is considered carcinogenic and must, therefore, be handled using cytotoxic precautions. People taking 
cidofovir
 should be advised to drink plenty of fluids to prevent renal failure. Monitor serum creatinine and urine protein concentrations within 48 hours before each dose. 
Probenecid
 is given before and following a 
cidofovir
 infusion to minimise the possibility of nephrotoxicity. 
Neutropoenia
, 
iritis
 and uveitis are also monitored during therapy.
Entecavir
 is available as tablets that are taken on an empty stomach. This improves absorption of the medicine. There is a possible risk of hepatitis B exacerbation after stopping this medicine—the person should be monitored carefully. Treatment should not be stopped in people with advanced disease or cirrhosis as there is an increased risk of hepatic decompensation.
Famciclovir
 is available as tablets and requires adequate fluid consumption to prevent renal impairment. Use cautiously in people with renal or liver impairment. It can be used in the treatment of the disease and in its prophylaxis. In the treatment of acute attacks, the faster the therapy is initiated the more successful the results; occasionally, the condition may be completely aborted with the incidence of recurrences lessened. As for 
aciclovir
, consumption of 
famciclovir
 should start at least 48 hours after onset of a shingles rash; otherwise, little benefit is gained from treatment. In people with frequent recurrences, suppression can be obtained by continued treatment for several months.
Ganciclovir is given orally in the form of capsules and by intravenous injection. Oral ganciclovir is taken with or after food to maximise absorption. This drug should be handled using cytotoxic precautions, as it is considered carcinogenic. 
Neutropoenia
 and adverse renal effects can occur during the first few weeks of treatment, which are dose dependent and reversible. Monitor the complete blood count, electrolyte levels and renal function at baseline and during therapy.
Telbivudine
 is given as a once-daily tablet dose. People need to be informed that the medicine may cause tingling, numbness, muscle aches and pain. These may be indications of arthralgia, myalgia and myopathy. The dose should be decreased in renal impairment. As there is also the risk of hepatitis B exacerbation if treatment is stopped, people need to be monitored carefully during therapy.
Valaciclovir
 is available in tablet form and is consumed with plenty of fluids to prevent renal impairment. As for 
aciclovir
, consumption of 
valaciclovir
 should start at least 48 hours after onset of a shingles rash; otherwise, little benefit is gained from treatment. Use this drug cautiously in people with renal impairment. It is not recommended for people with HIV infection or in bone marrow or organ transplant recipients. People should be advised to consume at least 1.5 L of fluid each day during treatment with 
valaciclovir
.
Valganciclovir
 is also available in tablet form. During treatment, a complete blood count is taken at baseline and during maintenance treatment. Electrolyte levels, renal function and liver enzyme levels are also monitored regularly. 
76

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide26.xml
Antimetabolites
The antimetabolite 
flucytosine
 
is used to treat various systemic fungal infections, including those involving 
C. 
albicans
. Some fungi are more susceptible than others, and resistance has been reported.
Mechanism of action
Flucytosine
, like some of the antivirals, is a 
prodrug
. 
As an antimetabolite it is 
converted in fungal cells 
into its active form, 5-fluorouracil (a substituted pyrimidine), and incorporated in RNA, making it defective. 
Mammalian cells cannot activate 
flucytosine
. The medicine is 
fungistatic
,
 is usually given by intravenous infusion and is well distributed in all body compartments. 5-Fluorouracil and other antimetabolites are used in the management of some cancers (see Chapter 80). 
Flucytosine
 capsules are not marketed in Australia but they may be obtained through the Special Access Scheme. 
Common adverse effects 
As it is specific to fungi, 
adverse effects are few. 
Nausea, vomiting and diarrhoea are seen occasionally. 
Flucytosine
 is excreted unchanged by the kidneys, and care must be taken in people with renal insufficiency, as blood levels may reach toxic levels due to an increase in half-life. 
Clinical considerations
Flucytosine
 is available as an 
injection preparation
. As resistance to 
flucytosine
 may develop rapidly and it has 
a narrow spectrum of action
, 
it is used in combination with another antifungal agent. Monitor the blood count, and liver and renal functions, at baseline and then regularly during treatment.
Concentration monitoring of 
flucytosine
 is important in renal impairment when used in conjunction with amphotericin. For individuals who do not have renal impairment, the dose interval of therapy for 
flucytosine
 is every six hours. However, the dose interval is progressively increased with increasing severity of renal impairment to reduce the risk of haematological adverse effects, such as anaemia, 
leukopoenia
 and agranulocytosis. 
Flucytosine
 should be kept at 15–25 °C. It forms fluorouracil at temperatures above 25 °C and it can precipitate when kept below 15 °C. 
98

## ppt/slides/slide4.xml
Objectives for Part A
General information about antibiotics 
Define the term ‘synergistic’ and explain why it is important in antibiotic and antiviral therapy

Briefly discuss 
antibiotics
 with respect to: 
The definition
Their spectrum of activity,

## ppt/slides/slide5.xml
Synergism
A joint action of two drugs in such a manner that one supplements or enhances the action of the other to produce 
an effect greater than that which may be obtained with either one of the drugs
 in equivalent quantity or produce effects that could not be obtained with any safe quantity of either drug, or both. 

## ppt/slides/slide6.xml
Sterilants
, disinfectants and antiseptics
Antiseptic	
A topical agent that can help prevent infection by inhibiting the growth of infectious agents. 
Example: 70% alcohol, 6% hydrogen peroxide 
Specific example: Cetrimide, which is used for cleaning wounds
Sterilant (used on surfaces, NOT on skin)
An agent that kills all forms of life, including transmissible agents present on a surface, contained in a fluid, or in biological culture media. A sterilant is capable of completely eliminating or destroying of all forms of microbial life, including spores. 
Example: Ethylene oxide (
EtO
)
Disinfectant (used on surfaces, NOT on skin)
An agent that is less capable than a sterilant in killing organisms. 
Example: Glutaraldehyde

## ppt/slides/slide7.xml
Viruses, bacteria and fungi
Not to scale
See details for sizes
Bacteria are about 0.4 micron to 2 micron or more.
A micron is one millionth of a meter
Fungal cells are about 2-10 microns.
Viruses range in size from 
0.02
 to 0.25 micron. They are 
submicroscopic
, needing an electron microscope to be studied.

## ppt/slides/slide9.xml


## ppt/slides/slide10.xml
Chemotherapy —  drugs with selective toxicity
The word ‘chemotherapy’ first used in the early 1900s 
The first chemotherapeutic agent was arsphenamine, an arsenic compound discovered in 1909 and used to treat syphilis. 
Later followed by sulphonamides and penicillin. 
Some authors still describe chemotherapy as “an agent that has selective toxicity against an invader (a virus, a bacterium, a parasite or a cancerous cell), and causes little damage to the normal cells of the host organism”. 

## ppt/slides/slide11.xml
Bactericidal vs bacteriostatic
Death of the susceptible microbe = bactericidal action
Death may result in endotoxins being released  
Generally those that inhibit the formation of a cell wall or disrupt the cell membrane 
Inhibit the growth and, therefore, inhibit the spread of the microbial population = bacteriostatic action. 
Enables the host’s immune system to rid the invading organisms. 
Generally those that inhibit protein synthesis or interfere with metabolic processes are bacteriostatic
…but some bacteriostatic medicines are bactericidal at higher doses

## ppt/slides/slide12.xml
Spectrum of activity
The number of species of microbes that are sensitive to the action of the antimicrobial agent is said to represent its spectrum of activity. 
Narrow spectrum antibiotics
Penicillin G (effective against only gram positive bacteria)
Broad spectrum antibiotics
Tetracyclines (effective against both Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria, mycoplasma, chlamydia and some protozoa)
A narrow-spectrum antimicrobial agent, may be more efficacious against a specific bacteria

## ppt/slides/slide13.xml
Time-dependent antibiotics
These antimicrobial agents need time to produce their therapeutic effect.
Consider the analogy of the chemical needing time to soak in. No matter how much antibiotic there is, it still takes x number of days. 
An increase in dose produces no increase in effect but an increase in time does
Examples of antimicrobial agents that have a time-dependent activity on microorganisms are the 
β-
lactams (e.g. penicillins), macrolides (e.g. erythromycin) and glycoproteins (e.g. vancomycin, teicoplanin). 

## ppt/slides/slide14.xml
Concentration/dose dependent antibiotics
The higher the peak blood level of medicine, the better the antibacterial effect achieved
Examples of antimicrobial agents that have a concentration-dependent activity on microorganisms are the 
quinolones
 (e.g. nor
floxacin
, cipro
floxacin
) and aminoglycosides (e.g. gentamicin, tobramycin).

## ppt/slides/slide16.xml
Side effects of antibiotics
Antibiotics result in killing off some natural flora
(Take with food)
This may result in a proliferation of other bacteria (and superinfections)
This disturbs the balance between good and bad bacteria.
***
 
Certain pathogenic organisms may then proliferate to a point where a serious secondary illness arises—a superinfection. 
…or Candida albicans infection (candidiasis)
mucous membranes of the mouth,  gastrointestinal tract and/or vagina. 
Rash, local itchiness 
Diarrhoea if in the GIT
Hypersensitivity is also a frequent side effect

## ppt/slides/slide17.xml
Side effects of antibiotics
***
 antibiotics disturbing the balance of flora
Nearly all antibiotics can cause antibiotic-associated diarrhoea. Antibiotics most commonly involved include:
Macrolides, such as clarithromycin
Cephalosporins, such as cefdinir and cefpodoxime
Fluoroquinolones, such as ciprofloxacin and levofloxacin
Penicillins, such as amoxicillin and ampicillin
One of the most serious causes of antibiotic-associated diarrhoea is 
Clostridioides
 difficile overgrowth. C. difficile bacteria create toxins that injure the lining of the intestine.
Treatment: discontinue the offending antibiotic and maintain adequate hydration. 
Question: would you suggest loperamide?

(an 
allergic
 response; anaphylaxis if extreme)

## ppt/slides/slide20.xml
Factors related to antibiotic resistance
Overuse: humans and animals
Under dosage/poor compliance
Extended prophylaxis
Use of non-suitable antibiotics
Self-administration of leftover drugs
Prolonged hospitalisation
Previous treatment with a variety of antibiotics
Nursing home/child care centre
Indwelling devices

## ppt/slides/slide21.xml
Don’t contribute to antibiotic resistance!
Educate patients (raising awareness of the dangers of taking antibiotics unnecessarily)
On self help measures for minor illnesses such as coughs, colds, sore throats and ear infections. Antibiotics don’t help these!
 
Failure to conform to an appropriate dosage regimen by not taking a full dose 
Use correct hand washing technique
Regular cleaning of clinical areas
Encourage use of disposable equipment or correct decontamination of equipment if needed
Regular training and updates to ensure best practice across a range of areas including antibiotic prescribing and infection control. 
Improper use of antimicrobial agents has led to bacteria acquiring resistance. 
Using broad-spectrum antibiotics to treat an infection when the causative microbe is sensitive to a narrow-spectrum medicine 
Antibiotic sensitivity testing indicates the appropriate medicines 
Take a culture and sensitivity prior to administration of antibiotics to ensure that the antibiotic chosen is the best choice

## ppt/slides/slide22.xml
Microscopy, culture and sensitivity (MC&S) 
Microscopy: The sample can be viewed under a microscope within minutes of arriving at the laboratory. This enables a quick initial report, such as ‘Gram positive cocci seen’, to be telephoned to the clinician if necessary. Combined with an accurate clinical picture, this may be enough to initiate targeted treatment.
Culture: A sample from the original culture is grown to create a pure sample to enable further identification of an organism.
Sensitivity: A pure culture is seeded onto an agar plate containing discs saturated with various antibiotics. If the organism grows up to a disc it is resistant to that antibiotic; if there is a clear zone around the disc, it is susceptible.

## ppt/slides/slide38.xml
Interference with metabolic processes
Affect folic acid
Without folic acid, the bacteria cannot produce the proteins necessary for growth. 
Microbes must synthesise their own folic acid, whereas humans can use folic acid present in their diet. 
Example: 
Sulfonamides

onamides

*

or

; interfere with folic acid 
Bacteriostatic action: inhibits microbial growth 
All microbial stores of folic acid need to be depleted so this can take some time.

sulf
onamides
 
High level of toxicity
: drink with plenty of water, monitor folate status, take the liver enzymes and kidney function tests!
Diarrhoea,
Crystalluria (as a consequence of these medicines becoming insoluble in the urine due to the acidity of this environment) 
Hypersensitivity

## ppt/slides/slide41.xml
Tetra
cycline
s
Often end in the suffix – 
cycline
Tetracyclines are broad-spectrum, bacteriostatic antibiotics that work at the ribosomes to decrease protein synthesis. 
Demeclo
cycline 
is natural but all others (
*doxy
cycline*
, *
m
ino
cycline
*
, etc) are either semisynthetic or synthetic derivatives. 
Tetra
cycline
s are generally effective against the Plasmodium spp. (protozoa) that cause malaria, many Mycoplasma (bacteria), Chlamydia (bacteria), 
rickettsiae
 (bacteria) and spirochaetes (bacteria), and some amoebae (protozoa), however many bacteria have become resistant .

## ppt/slides/slide42.xml

cycline
s
Common adverse effects:
Gastrointestinal disturbances (nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, anorexia, abdominal pain),

Superinfections 
Clinical considerations: 
Poorly absorbed but still therapeutically effective if taken one hour before or two hours after meals
Binds to calcium in bones and teeth. – DO NOT TAKE WITH CALCIUM SUPPLEMENTS. WILL STAIN NEWLY FORMING TEETH 
Tetra
cyclines
 may cause photosensitivity

## ppt/slides/slide43.xml
Aminoglycosides
Normally end in – 
mycin
 or 
micin
 (the 
meaning
 is ‘derived from a fungus’, so 
other antibiotics have this suffix
)
Gentamicin and Neomycin 
Mechanism of action 
Inhibiting protein synthesis and, therefore, microbial growth
While inhibition of protein synthesis is normally bacteriostatic, these are so potent at therapeutic that they are bactericidal

## ppt/slides/slide44.xml
Aminoglycosides
Broad spectrum antibiotics
Wide range of mostly Gram-negative bacteria 
Used when microbes are found to be resistant to cheaper and less toxic antibacterial agents. 
Common adverse effects: 
High level of toxicity
The kidneys (
nephrotoxicity
), the eighth cranial nerve (
ototoxicity
) and the neuromuscular junction (neuromuscular paralysis).  
Most are IV except for neomycin which can sterilize bowel prior to surgery
Gentamycin and neomycin are probably 
more important as topical antibiotics 
where they are used after burns, and open wounds. They are often combined with other topical antibiotics

## ppt/slides/slide46.xml
Inhibiting bacterial DNA

)

(

*, *levo

)
The quinolones are a family of synthetic broad-spectrum antibiotics
Fluoroquinolones are broad-spectrum antibiotics effective for both gram-negative and gram-positive bacteria 
Important in treatment of serious bacterial infections, especially nosocomial (hospital-acquired) infections
Quinolones exert their antibacterial effect by preventing bacterial DNA from unwinding and duplicating

## ppt/slides/slide50.xml
Objectives
Briefly discuss fungi with respect to their similarities to human cells, differences to bacterial cells and the specific disorders they cause.
‘Antifungal agents are toxic to humans.’ Discuss this statement including examples of the types of toxicity and measures to help decrease the effects of the toxicity. 
Briefly discuss the following antifungal agents including their clinical applications, side effects and cautions
