## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide20.xml
66
Viruses do not fit the normal description of life. 
Perhaps it is better to think of viruses as infectious agents that enter cells and interfere with their normal function. A virus itself is not a cell, but a strand of either DNA or RNA, which has a protein coat and sometimes a lipid envelope. There is no cell membrane or cytoplasm. When a virus particle, a 
virion
, enters a cell, it uses the host cell enzymes to synthesise more of its nucleic acid and the components of its 
extranucleic
 structures. The viral nucleic acid can code for specific viral enzymes. A virus employs at least some of the existing metabolic machinery in the host cell for its own use. It is, therefore, very difficult to come up with a medicine that can destroy the virus without affecting the host cell. The process of infecting a host cell, viral synthesis and release of new viruses is represented in Figure 77.1. 
Most antiviral agents target the differences that exist between viral nucleic acids or enzymes, and those of the human host. Common sites of antiviral drug action are represented in Figure 77.2. 

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide21.xml
HIV INFECTIONS 
The human immunodeficiency virus (HIV), is a retrovirus and is caused mainly by two viral strains, HIV-1 and HIV-2. HIV-1 is found worldwide, whereas HIV-2 infections are commonest in parts of Africa and India. 
A 
retrovirus
 is a single-stranded 
RNA virus
 that stores its nucleic acid in the form of an 
mRNA
 genome 
Once inside the host cell 
cytoplasm
, the virus uses its own 
reverse transcriptase
 enzyme to produce 
DNA
 from its 
RNA
 genome, the reverse of the usual pattern, thus 
retro
 (backwards). 
This new DNA is then 
incorporated
 into the host cell 
genome
 by an 
integrase
 enzyme, at which point the retroviral DNA is referred to as a 
provirus
. The host cell then treats the viral DNA as part of its own 
genome
, translating and transcribing the viral genes along with the cell's own genes, producing the proteins required to assemble new copies of the virus. It is difficult to detect the virus until it has infected the host. At that point, the infection will persist indefinitely.
The antiviral agents used in the suppression of HIV are mostly effective against the HIV-1 strain
. 
The virus infects the CD4 cells (a group of helper T lymphocytes that possess the CD4 membrane receptor), thus decreasing the effectiveness of the cell-mediated immune systems. 
Two of the most  important drug groups used to suppress HIV are reverse transcriptase inhibitors and HIV protease inhibitors. The viral enzymes reverse transcriptase and protease are more susceptible to AIDS medicines than are human enzymes.
 Humans do not have the reverse transcriptase enzyme; however, these enzyme inhibitors can slightly affect other enzymes involved in human nucleic acid metabolism. 
A number of other medicines are also available for use in the management of HIV and AIDS, including 
fusion inhibitors, 
74

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide22.xml
DNA polymerase inhibitors
This group of antivirals includes 
aciclovir
, 
cidofovir
, 
entecavir
, 
famciclovir
, 
ganciclovir
, 
telbivudine
, 
valaciclovir
 
and 
valganciclovir
. The action of 
foscarnet
, at least in part, also places it in this grouping. 
Telbivudine
 is only available in Australia.
Mechanism of action
Aciclovir
 (sometimes spelt acyclovir) is a guanine derivative that acts as a 
prodrug
. It must be phosphorylated before it becomes active. It is this property that makes 
aciclovir
 such a useful antiviral agent in at least one viral infection—that is, the one caused by herpes virus type I. If a cell is infected with this virus, the viral enzyme thymidine kinase is present in the cell. This enzyme converts 
aciclovir
 into acyclovir 
monophosphate. Other enzymes, in turn, convert this into 
aciclovir
 triphosphate, which is the active form of the drug. 
It forms a complex with the enzyme DNA polymerase, which is needed for DNA replication, and inactivates it. This process occurs only in infected cells, and much higher concentrations of the drug are needed to affect host DNA polymerase. 
Aciclovir
, therefore, selectively harms only the virus in infected cells. 
As already stated, this medicine is best for the herpes type I virus. It does, however, have some deleterious effects on other viruses, such as varicella, herpes zoster (shingles) and Epstein–Barr virus. This medicine is not a cure for herpes but will merely suppress the symptoms and inhibit further replication of the virus. As with many viral infections, the virus can cause what is known as a latent or dormant infection, which can recur at a later date. It appears that the virus goes into ‘hiding’ away from the body’s immune system, and when circumstances permit it reappears to cause a clinical infection. 
Famciclovir
, like 
aciclovir
, is a 
prodrug
 and is converted into the active form, 
penciclovir
, within the body. The action of 
penciclovir
 is identical to that of 
aciclovir
 but has certain advantages in that it is better absorbed from the gut and has a longer half-life than the active form of 
aciclovir
. Compared to 
aciclovir
, less frequent dosing is required, resulting in better treatment adherence, especially during long-term therapy. 
Famciclovir
 has less activity on the type II virus than on the type I form. This relative lack of activity on the type II virus may lessen its usefulness in the treatment of genital herpes. 
Famciclovir
 is particularly useful in the treatment of shingles. Treatment must be given within 48 hours of appearance of the rash. 
Valaciclovir
 is a derivative of the amino acid 
valine
 and 
aciclovir
. (
Valaciclovir
 is, therefore, a pro-
prodrug
.) It has a very high bioavailability and a high hepatic first pass and is converted into 
aciclovir
. The advantage over 
aciclovir
 is that treatment with 
valaciclovir
 can attain blood concentrations of 
aciclovir
 equivalent to those attained when 
aciclovir
 is administered 
parenterally
. As 
aciclovir
 has probably fewer adverse effects and drug interactions, 
valaciclovir
 may prove to be one of the best of the antiviral agents for the treatment of herpes zoster, for the treatment and suppression of genital herpes, and for prophylaxis of CMV infection and disease in immunocompromised people. 
Ganciclovir, like 
aciclovir
, is a guanine derivative that can be used to treat CMV infections in immunocompromised individuals, such as people with AIDS. 
Valganciclovir
 is a 
valine
 derivative of ganciclovir and acts as a 
prodrug
. 
Cidofovir
 is a cytidine derivative, which is also used for CMV infections in immunocompromised patients. Medicines from this group have an inhibitory effect on DNA polymerase of CMV that is 600–800 times greater than that on the human enzyme. 
Telbividine
 is indicated in the treatment of chronic hepatitis B in nucleoside-naive people with compensated liver disease. It is a thymidine nucleoside analogue that competes with the natural thymidine nucleoside, thymidine triphosphate, and inhibits hepatitis B DNA polymerase. This action disrupts the replication of viral DNA. 
Entecavir
 is also indicated in the therapy of chronic hepatitis B in adults and children 16 years and older with active liver inflammation. It is a 
guanosine
 nucleoside analogue that is phosphorylated to 
entecavir
 triphosphate. It competes with the natural substrate, 
deoxyguanosine
 triphosphate, to inhibit the activity of hepatitis B DNA polymerase. 
Common adverse effects 
As might be expected from the mode of action, 
famciclovir
, 
valaciclovir
 and 
aciclovir
 have minimal adverse effects. Common adverse effects include headache, nausea and vomiting. 
Ganciclovir and 
cidofovir
 often cause 
neutropoenia
 and are potentially carcinogenic. Ganciclovir can induce aplastic anaemia. Hence, they are used only in life-threatening infections. 
Cidofovir
 is nephrotoxic and should not be used in people with renal impairment, or in combination with or after the use of nephrotoxic medicines. The concomitant use of 
probenecid
 
helps prevent nephrotoxic reactions. 
Clinical considerations 
In genital herpes, 
aciclovir
 speeds up the healing rate of the lesions and decreases the associated pain. It can be given orally, intravenously or applied as an ointment. Topical 
aciclovir
 is useful to treat herpes infections of the eyes and lips. However, topical 
aciclovir
 is considered less effective than the oral form of treatment. 
Aciclovir
 is administered intravenously to treat immunocompromised people as well as individuals with herpes simplex encephalitis. As oral 
aciclovir
 has a comparatively low bioavailability, parenteral administration is often needed in severe viral infections. Missed 
aciclovir
 tablets may produce blood levels at less than the minimum effective concentration. This can lead to ineffectiveness of the treatment. People need to be counselled about the need to comply with oral treatment to ensure maximum effectiveness. During 
aciclovir
 therapy, it is important to ensure that the person drinks adequate fluids (at least 2.5 L/day) to prevent renal impairment. Adequate hydration is particularly important for intravenous administration and when high doses are 
used. Tablets can be dispersed in water or swallowed whole. Advise people to start treatment as soon as possible if using 
aciclovir
 for shingles. Administration should start at least 48 hours after onset of the shingles rash; otherwise, little benefit is gained from treatment. Although early treatment of shingles reduces the duration of acute neuralgia, 
aciclovir
 treatment does not lessen the duration of 
postherpetic
 pain. Use cautiously in people with neurological disorders, renal disease or dehydration.
Cidofovir
 is given intravenously using veins with good flow. It is considered carcinogenic and must, therefore, be handled using cytotoxic precautions. People taking 
cidofovir
 should be advised to drink plenty of fluids to prevent renal failure. Monitor serum creatinine and urine protein concentrations within 48 hours before each dose. 
Probenecid
 is given before and following a 
cidofovir
 infusion to minimise the possibility of nephrotoxicity. 
Neutropoenia
, 
iritis
 and uveitis are also monitored during therapy.
Entecavir
 is available as tablets that are taken on an empty stomach. This improves absorption of the medicine. There is a possible risk of hepatitis B exacerbation after stopping this medicine—the person should be monitored carefully. Treatment should not be stopped in people with advanced disease or cirrhosis as there is an increased risk of hepatic decompensation.
Famciclovir
 is available as tablets and requires adequate fluid consumption to prevent renal impairment. Use cautiously in people with renal or liver impairment. It can be used in the treatment of the disease and in its prophylaxis. In the treatment of acute attacks, the faster the therapy is initiated the more successful the results; occasionally, the condition may be completely aborted with the incidence of recurrences lessened. As for 
aciclovir
, consumption of 
famciclovir
 should start at least 48 hours after onset of a shingles rash; otherwise, little benefit is gained from treatment. In people with frequent recurrences, suppression can be obtained by continued treatment for several months.
Ganciclovir is given orally in the form of capsules and by intravenous injection. Oral ganciclovir is taken with or after food to maximise absorption. This drug should be handled using cytotoxic precautions, as it is considered carcinogenic. 
Neutropoenia
 and adverse renal effects can occur during the first few weeks of treatment, which are dose dependent and reversible. Monitor the complete blood count, electrolyte levels and renal function at baseline and during therapy.
Telbivudine
 is given as a once-daily tablet dose. People need to be informed that the medicine may cause tingling, numbness, muscle aches and pain. These may be indications of arthralgia, myalgia and myopathy. The dose should be decreased in renal impairment. As there is also the risk of hepatitis B exacerbation if treatment is stopped, people need to be monitored carefully during therapy.
Valaciclovir
 is available in tablet form and is consumed with plenty of fluids to prevent renal impairment. As for 
aciclovir
, consumption of 
valaciclovir
 should start at least 48 hours after onset of a shingles rash; otherwise, little benefit is gained from treatment. Use this drug cautiously in people with renal impairment. It is not recommended for people with HIV infection or in bone marrow or organ transplant recipients. People should be advised to consume at least 1.5 L of fluid each day during treatment with 
valaciclovir
.
Valganciclovir
 is also available in tablet form. During treatment, a complete blood count is taken at baseline and during maintenance treatment. Electrolyte levels, renal function and liver enzyme levels are also monitored regularly. 
76

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide26.xml
Antimetabolites
The antimetabolite 
flucytosine
 
is used to treat various systemic fungal infections, including those involving 
C. 
albicans
. Some fungi are more susceptible than others, and resistance has been reported.
Mechanism of action
Flucytosine
, like some of the antivirals, is a 
prodrug
. 
As an antimetabolite it is 
converted in fungal cells 
into its active form, 5-fluorouracil (a substituted pyrimidine), and incorporated in RNA, making it defective. 
Mammalian cells cannot activate 
flucytosine
. The medicine is 
fungistatic
,
 is usually given by intravenous infusion and is well distributed in all body compartments. 5-Fluorouracil and other antimetabolites are used in the management of some cancers (see Chapter 80). 
Flucytosine
 capsules are not marketed in Australia but they may be obtained through the Special Access Scheme. 
Common adverse effects 
As it is specific to fungi, 
adverse effects are few. 
Nausea, vomiting and diarrhoea are seen occasionally. 
Flucytosine
 is excreted unchanged by the kidneys, and care must be taken in people with renal insufficiency, as blood levels may reach toxic levels due to an increase in half-life. 
Clinical considerations
Flucytosine
 is available as an 
injection preparation
. As resistance to 
flucytosine
 may develop rapidly and it has 
a narrow spectrum of action
, 
it is used in combination with another antifungal agent. Monitor the blood count, and liver and renal functions, at baseline and then regularly during treatment.
Concentration monitoring of 
flucytosine
 is important in renal impairment when used in conjunction with amphotericin. For individuals who do not have renal impairment, the dose interval of therapy for 
flucytosine
 is every six hours. However, the dose interval is progressively increased with increasing severity of renal impairment to reduce the risk of haematological adverse effects, such as anaemia, 
leukopoenia
 and agranulocytosis. 
Flucytosine
 should be kept at 15–25 °C. It forms fluorouracil at temperatures above 25 °C and it can precipitate when kept below 15 °C.

## ppt/slides/slide4.xml
Objectives for Part A
General information about antibiotics 
Define the term ‘synergistic’ and explain why it is important in antibiotic and antiviral therapy
Identify the indications, contraindications and one example of an antiseptic, a sterilant, and a bacteriostatic agent.
Briefly discuss 
antibiotics
 with respect to: 
The definition
Their spectrum of activity, 
The mechanism by which they work (citing two different mechanisms), 
Their effect on the normal flora on skin, and mucous membranes of the GIT and vagina, relating these to their side effects
Briefly discuss the terms 'time dependent', 'dose dependent' and 'culture sensitivity' as they pertain to antibiotics. 
Identify two ways in which health professionals can help prevent the emergence of resistant strains of bacteria. 

## ppt/slides/slide5.xml
Synergism
A joint action of two drugs in such a manner that one supplements or enhances the action of the other to produce 
an effect greater than that which may be obtained with either one of the drugs
 in equivalent quantity or produce effects that could not be obtained with any safe quantity of either drug, or both. 

## ppt/slides/slide6.xml
Sterilants
, disinfectants and antiseptics
Antiseptic	
A topical agent that can help prevent infection by inhibiting the growth of infectious agents. 
Example: 70% alcohol, 6% hydrogen peroxide 
Specific example: Cetrimide, which is used for cleaning wounds
Sterilant (used on surfaces, NOT on skin)
An agent that kills all forms of life, including transmissible agents present on a surface, contained in a fluid, or in biological culture media. A sterilant is capable of completely eliminating or destroying of all forms of microbial life, including spores. 
Example: Ethylene oxide (
EtO
)
Disinfectant (used on surfaces, NOT on skin)
An agent that is less capable than a sterilant in killing organisms. 
Example: Glutaraldehyde

## ppt/slides/slide7.xml
Viruses, bacteria and fungi
Not to scale
See details for sizes
Bacteria are about 0.4 micron to 2 micron or more.
A micron is one millionth of a meter
Fungal cells are about 2-10 microns.
Viruses range in size from 
0.02
 to 0.25 micron. They are 
submicroscopic
, needing an electron microscope to be studied.

## ppt/slides/slide9.xml


## ppt/slides/slide10.xml
Chemotherapy —  drugs with selective toxicity
The word ‘chemotherapy’ first used in the early 1900s 
The first chemotherapeutic agent was arsphenamine, an arsenic compound discovered in 1909 and used to treat syphilis. 
Later followed by sulphonamides and penicillin. 
Some authors still describe chemotherapy as “an agent that has selective toxicity against an invader (a virus, a bacterium, a parasite or a cancerous cell), and causes little damage to the normal cells of the host organism”. 

## ppt/slides/slide11.xml
Bactericidal vs bacteriostatic
Death of the susceptible microbe = bactericidal action
Death may result in endotoxins being released  
Generally those that inhibit the formation of a cell wall or disrupt the cell membrane 
Inhibit the growth and, therefore, inhibit the spread of the microbial population = bacteriostatic action. 
Enables the host’s immune system to rid the invading organisms. 
Generally those that inhibit protein synthesis or interfere with metabolic processes are bacteriostatic
…but some bacteriostatic medicines are bactericidal at higher doses

## ppt/slides/slide12.xml
Spectrum of activity
The number of species of microbes that are sensitive to the action of the antimicrobial agent is said to represent its spectrum of activity. 
Narrow spectrum antibiotics
Penicillin G (effective against only gram positive bacteria)
Broad spectrum antibiotics
Tetracyclines (effective against both Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria, mycoplasma, chlamydia and some protozoa)
A narrow-spectrum antimicrobial agent, may be more efficacious against a specific bacteria

## ppt/slides/slide13.xml
Time-dependent antibiotics
These antimicrobial agents need time to produce their therapeutic effect.
Consider the analogy of the chemical needing time to soak in. No matter how much antibiotic there is, it still takes x number of days. 
An increase in dose produces no increase in effect but an increase in time does
Examples of antimicrobial agents that have a time-dependent activity on microorganisms are the 
β-
lactams (e.g. penicillins), macrolides (e.g. erythromycin) and glycoproteins (e.g. vancomycin, teicoplanin). 

## ppt/slides/slide14.xml
Concentration/dose dependent antibiotics
The higher the peak blood level of medicine, the better the antibacterial effect achieved
Examples of antimicrobial agents that have a concentration-dependent activity on microorganisms are the 
quinolones
 (e.g. nor
floxacin
, cipro
floxacin
) and aminoglycosides (e.g. gentamicin, tobramycin).

## ppt/slides/slide16.xml
Side effects of antibiotics
Antibiotics result in killing off some natural flora
(Take with food)
This may result in a proliferation of other bacteria (and superinfections)
This disturbs the balance between good and bad bacteria.
***
 
Certain pathogenic organisms may then proliferate to a point where a serious secondary illness arises—a superinfection. 
…or Candida albicans infection (candidiasis)
mucous membranes of the mouth,  gastrointestinal tract and/or vagina. 
Rash, local itchiness 
Diarrhoea if in the GIT
Hypersensitivity is also a frequent side effect

## ppt/slides/slide17.xml
Side effects of antibiotics
***
 antibiotics disturbing the balance of flora
Nearly all antibiotics can cause antibiotic-associated diarrhoea. Antibiotics most commonly involved include:
Macrolides, such as clarithromycin
Cephalosporins, such as cefdinir and cefpodoxime
Fluoroquinolones, such as ciprofloxacin and levofloxacin
Penicillins, such as amoxicillin and ampicillin
One of the most serious causes of antibiotic-associated diarrhoea is 
Clostridioides
 difficile overgrowth. C. difficile bacteria create toxins that injure the lining of the intestine.
Treatment: discontinue the offending antibiotic and maintain adequate hydration. 
Question: would you suggest loperamide?

Common side effects of antibiotics

(an 
allergic
 response; anaphylaxis if extreme)

## ppt/slides/slide20.xml
Factors related to antibiotic resistance
Overuse: humans and animals
Under dosage/poor compliance
Extended prophylaxis
Use of non-suitable antibiotics
Self-administration of leftover drugs
Prolonged hospitalisation
Previous treatment with a variety of antibiotics
Nursing home/child care centre
Indwelling devices

## ppt/slides/slide21.xml
Don’t contribute to antibiotic resistance!
Educate patients (raising awareness of the dangers of taking antibiotics unnecessarily)
On self help measures for minor illnesses such as coughs, colds, sore throats and ear infections. Antibiotics don’t help these!
 
Failure to conform to an appropriate dosage regimen by not taking a full dose 
Use correct hand washing technique
Regular cleaning of clinical areas
Encourage use of disposable equipment or correct decontamination of equipment if needed
Regular training and updates to ensure best practice across a range of areas including antibiotic prescribing and infection control. 
Improper use of antimicrobial agents has led to bacteria acquiring resistance. 
Using broad-spectrum antibiotics to treat an infection when the causative microbe is sensitive to a narrow-spectrum medicine 
Antibiotic sensitivity testing indicates the appropriate medicines 
Take a culture and sensitivity prior to administration of antibiotics to ensure that the antibiotic chosen is the best choice

## ppt/slides/slide22.xml
Microscopy, culture and sensitivity (MC&S) 
Microscopy: The sample can be viewed under a microscope within minutes of arriving at the laboratory. This enables a quick initial report, such as ‘Gram positive cocci seen’, to be telephoned to the clinician if necessary. Combined with an accurate clinical picture, this may be enough to initiate targeted treatment.
Culture: A sample from the original culture is grown to create a pure sample to enable further identification of an organism.
Sensitivity: A pure culture is seeded onto an agar plate containing discs saturated with various antibiotics. If the organism grows up to a disc it is resistant to that antibiotic; if there is a clear zone around the disc, it is susceptible.

## ppt/slides/slide27.xml
Gram Positive bacteria
 Thick layer of 
P
eptidoglycan
Gram 
P
ositive
 P
eptidoglycan (
P
urple)
Generally more easily treated with antibiotics
Our best defence is 
not 
our specific immunity - More susceptible to our innate immune system like enzymes in our secretions
Gram Negative bacteria
Thin layer of peptidoglycan and many lipopolysaccharides
Found mainly in the genitourinary and gastrointestinal tracts
Harder to treat with antibiotics and 
Often release endotoxin when dying 
More susceptible to acquired, specific immune system

## ppt/slides/slide32.xml
Peni
cillin
s
Bactericidal 
-
cillin
 = suffix denoting a drug from the penicillin or 
β-
lactam class of antibiotics
Derived from a genus of moulds called 
Penicillium
. 
Natural penicillins are designated by letters. 
*Penicillin V* (phenoxymethylpenicillin) is less potent than penicillin G. 
All other penicillins are either wholly or partially synthetic. *Amoxycillin*

## ppt/slides/slide33.xml
Peni
cillin
s
Broad spectrum penicillins include *amoxi
cillin*
 and ampi
cillin
 
Effective against numerous species of Gram-negative bacteria. 
Common adverse effects 
Gastrointestinal disturbances (nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, epigastric distress) and oedema
Hypersensitivity reactions including 
RASHES
HYPERSENSITIVITIES occur in about 10 per cent of individuals receiving 
β-
lactam antibiotics, but it is known that only about 10% of these are true allergies. Anaphylaxis occurs in 0.02 per cent. USE MACROLIDES AS AN ALTERNATIVE ANTIBIOTIC
Cross-sensitivity to 
ceph
alosporins and carbapenems may occur! 

## ppt/slides/slide34.xml

Just say no to:
Cephalosporins and
Carbapenems

## ppt/slides/slide35.xml
Ceph
alosporins
*
Cef
dinir* *
Ceph
alexin*
Semisynthetic derivatives of the antibiotic produced by the mould 
Ceph
alosporium
Are normally wider spectrum against Gram-negative bacteria and longer half-life than the penicillins (
β-
lactam antibiotics)
Five or more generations of cephalosporins, most are delivered parenterally as intravenous or intramuscular injections 
but cefdinir and cephalexin can be taken orally, hence they are in the top 300 most prescribed medications
.

## ppt/slides/slide36.xml
Ceph
alosporins
Common adverse effects
Gastrointestinal disturbances (diarrhoea, nausea, antibiotic-associated colitis), especially if taken orally 
Hypersensitivity reactions and 
Pain on intramuscular injection if IM. 
People with a history of an urticarial or anaphylactic reaction should avoid taking 
penicillins, 
cephalosporins
 and carbapenems.
People in renal failure must avoid cephalosporins although Cefdinir has less renal toxicity than other cephalosporins.
Do not combine with aminoglycosides

## ppt/slides/slide38.xml
Interference with metabolic processes
Affect folic acid
Without folic acid, the bacteria cannot produce the proteins necessary for growth. 
Microbes must synthesise their own folic acid, whereas humans can use folic acid present in their diet. 
Example: 
Sulfonamides

## ppt/slides/slide39.xml
Sulf
onamides
Sulf
onamides
 (e.g. 
*
sulph
amethoxazole
*
) suffix: 
sulf
 
or 
sulph
; interfere with folic acid 
Bacteriostatic action: inhibits microbial growth 
All microbial stores of folic acid need to be depleted so this can take some time. 
Adverse effects associated with the use of 
sulf
onamides
 
High level of toxicity
: drink with plenty of water, monitor folate status, take the liver enzymes and kidney function tests!
Diarrhoea,
Crystalluria (as a consequence of these medicines becoming insoluble in the urine due to the acidity of this environment) 
Hypersensitivity

s (

, *

).

## ppt/slides/slide41.xml
Tetra
cycline
s
Often end in the suffix – 
cycline
Tetracyclines are broad-spectrum, bacteriostatic antibiotics that work at the ribosomes to decrease protein synthesis. 
Demeclo
cycline 
is natural but all others (
*doxy
cycline*
, *
m
ino
cycline
*
, etc) are either semisynthetic or synthetic derivatives. 
Tetra
cycline
s are generally effective against the Plasmodium spp. (protozoa) that cause malaria, many Mycoplasma (bacteria), Chlamydia (bacteria), 
rickettsiae
 (bacteria) and spirochaetes (bacteria), and some amoebae (protozoa), however many bacteria have become resistant .

## ppt/slides/slide42.xml
Tetra
cycline
s
Common adverse effects:
Gastrointestinal disturbances (nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, anorexia, abdominal pain), 
Hypersensitivity reactions (skin rashes, discolouration of the nails, photosensitivity) and 
Superinfections 
Clinical considerations: 
Poorly absorbed but still therapeutically effective if taken one hour before or two hours after meals
Binds to calcium in bones and teeth. – DO NOT TAKE WITH CALCIUM SUPPLEMENTS. WILL STAIN NEWLY FORMING TEETH 
Tetra
cyclines
 may cause photosensitivity

## ppt/slides/slide43.xml
Aminoglycosides
Normally end in – 
mycin
 or 
micin
 (the 
meaning
 is ‘derived from a fungus’, so 
other antibiotics have this suffix
)
Gentamicin and Neomycin 
Mechanism of action 
Inhibiting protein synthesis and, therefore, microbial growth
While inhibition of protein synthesis is normally bacteriostatic, these are so potent at therapeutic that they are bactericidal

## ppt/slides/slide44.xml
Aminoglycosides
Broad spectrum antibiotics
Wide range of mostly Gram-negative bacteria 
Used when microbes are found to be resistant to cheaper and less toxic antibacterial agents. 
Common adverse effects: 
High level of toxicity
The kidneys (
nephrotoxicity
), the eighth cranial nerve (
ototoxicity
) and the neuromuscular junction (neuromuscular paralysis).  
Most are IV except for neomycin which can sterilize bowel prior to surgery
Gentamycin and neomycin are probably 
more important as topical antibiotics 
where they are used after burns, and open wounds. They are often combined with other topical antibiotics

 *Azithromycin*

 means derived from a fungus)

Lin
cosamides
*C
lin
damycin*
Can be used with other medications for malaria 

## ppt/slides/slide46.xml
Inhibiting bacterial DNA
Fluoroquinolones (
Quinalones
)
Most end in the suffix 
floxacin
 
(
*Cipro
floxacin
*, *levo
floxacin*
)
The quinolones are a family of synthetic broad-spectrum antibiotics
Fluoroquinolones are broad-spectrum antibiotics effective for both gram-negative and gram-positive bacteria 
Important in treatment of serious bacterial infections, especially nosocomial (hospital-acquired) infections
Quinolones exert their antibacterial effect by preventing bacterial DNA from unwinding and duplicating

## ppt/slides/slide47.xml
Nitroimi
dazole
 class
Metroni
dazole
 (Flagyl)
Metroni
dazole
 is of the nitroimi
dazole
 class. It disrupts the DNA of microbial cells
Antibiotic and antiprotozoal medication
Pelvic inflammatory disease, 
endocarditis,
 bacterial vaginosis.
Effective for dracunculiasis, 
giardiasis,
 trichomoniasis, and amebiasis. 
Effective on Clostridium difficile 
(from antibiotic use)
Oral, topical and IV
Common side effects include nausea, a metallic taste, loss of appetite, and headaches. Occasionally seizures or allergies to the medication may occur.
Pregnancy category B2

## ppt/slides/slide50.xml
Objectives
Briefly discuss fungi with respect to their similarities to human cells, differences to bacterial cells and the specific disorders they cause.
‘Antifungal agents are toxic to humans.’ Discuss this statement including examples of the types of toxicity and measures to help decrease the effects of the toxicity. 
Briefly discuss the following antifungal agents including their clinical applications, side effects and cautions 
Azole
 antifungals: Clotrim
azole
, *
Fluco
nazole
*, *Ketoco
nazole
* 
*Nystatin* (a polyene)

## ppt/slides/slide51.xml
Types of fungal infections
A fungal infection is termed a mycosis. Types:
Systemic or deep mycoses (normally soil saprophytes)
Subcutaneous mycoses (normally from soil saprophytes)
Cutaneous mycoses example: athlete’s foot. The fungi look like small worms burrowing beneath the skin
Latin tinea, a worm
Superficial mycoses. These occur when only the very outermost layers of the epidermis and hair are involved. 
Opportunistic mycoses example: candidiasis (also called 
candidosis
, 
moniliasis
 or thrush), which is caused by Candida albicans, a normal resident of the flora

## ppt/slides/slide52.xml
A subcutaneous mycosis 

## ppt/slides/slide53.xml
Fungi are similar in structure to mammalian cells (both are eukaryotic) 

## ppt/slides/slide54.xml
Measures to decrease the toxic effects of antifungal agents
Hepatotoxicity
Drink plenty of water
Frequent LFT (liver function tests) to note liver toxicity
Avoid alcohol, paracetamol and other drugs that are toxic to the liver
Renal toxicity
Drink plenty of water
Frequent UEC (urea electrolyte and creatinine) to note early signs of kidney toxicity
Avoid NSAIDs and aspirin, and other drugs that are toxic to the kidneys

## ppt/slides/slide55.xml
Other challenges with fungi  
Also, many pathogenic fungi exhibit a phenomenon known as dimorphism, in which two different types of growth forms exist. 
Phenotypic switching
 (a.k.a. phenotypic dimorphism) is switching between two cell-types. An example is Candida albicans, which, when it infects host tissue, switches from the usual unicellular yeast-like form into an invasive, multicellular filamentous form

## ppt/slides/slide56.xml
Most antifungal agents target the differences in the cell membrane
Mammalian cells have a cholesterol-rich cell membrane, 
Fungal cells have a membrane that is primarily composed of ergosterol.

eg.

eg.

eg.

## ppt/slides/slide58.xml
Inhibits sterol synthesis
Azole
 antifungal agents (note: there are some antibiotics (anti bacterial agents) with this suffix
Ergosterol is an essential component of fungal cell membranes but not of mammalian cells. Two mechanisms
Inhibits the formation of ergosterol, which disrupts the synthesis of the cell membrane. 
Decreases fungal cell elimination of hydrogen peroxide, which builds up intracellularly and kills the cell. 
Clotrim
azole
Topical and throat lozenges 
Treats vaginal yeast infections, oral thrush, diaper rash, and ringworm (
atheletes
 foot and jock itch)

## ppt/slides/slide59.xml
Fungal infections of the nail
(onychomycosis)
Topical agents
This one (ciclopirox) is generally ineffective (<60%) for fungal infections
Clotrim
azole 
is more effective (up to 80% completely resolved if nails were drilled every two weeks in addition to 1% clotrimazole)
Systemic antifungals
Allylamines - terbinafine 
6 month treatment – very toxic

## ppt/slides/slide60.xml
The 
Azole 
antifungals 
(continued): Flucon
azole 
and Ketocon
azole
*Fluco
nazole*
Effective against candidiasis, blastomycosis, 
coccidiodomycosis
, cryptococcosis, histoplasmosis, dermatophytosis, and pityriasis versicolor.
Oral or IV
Side effects include: Vomiting, diarrhea, rash, and increased liver enzymes. 
(Monitor liver enzymes!)
Not to be used in pregnancy: Category D
*Ketoco
nazole*
: 
Topical
 (Nizoral cream and shampoo) is still available but the oral medication was dysregulated due to liver toxicity.

## ppt/slides/slide61.xml
Polyene antifungal 
agents 
Mechanism of action 
*Nystatin* 
(oral suspension, topical vaginal and oral tablet) is a polyene that binds to ergosterol and disrupts fungal cell membranes, upsetting normal membrane permeability. 
Oral nystatin is often used as a preventive treatment in people who are at risk for fungal infections of the intestines, such as AIDS patients with a low CD4+ count and people receiving chemotherapy. There is no absorption of nystatin. 
Common adverse effects – Nystatin is not absorbed (0%) but can cause allergic reactions, hypersensitivity reactions, itching, rash and burning sensations

## ppt/slides/slide64.xml
Objectives for antiviral medications, Part 3
Discuss viruses with respect to their structure, the mechanisms by which they invade human cells and four examples of disorders they can cause.
The most important antiviral agents target four main viruses or groups of viruses targeted by antivirals: 
The herpes viruses
The influenza viruses (the flu) 
The hepatitis viruses and 
The human immunodeficiency virus
(also SARS-CoV-2 (COVID19) if any are approved)
For each of those viruses, cite one example of an effective antiviral medication, the molecular target of that medication and the most important clinical considerations.

)
Human Immunodeficiency virus that causes AIDS

## ppt/slides/slide66.xml
VIRUSES
Acellular: no nucleus, cytoplasm, organelles
Obligate intracellular parasites: replication requires host cell
Contain either RNA or DNA (never both)
Replication often disrupts or kills host cell
Nanometres(nm) divided by 1000 = microns

## ppt/slides/slide70.xml
Influenza: Neuraminidase inhibitors
Respiratory tract infection but NOT the common cold 
Two types of influenza virus—A and B (rarely C)
Neuraminidase inhibitors 
*Oselt
amivir
* (oral) and Zan
amivir
 (aerosol) stop the enzyme that the virus particles use to exit our cells. Thus, there is a decrease in the release of new viral particles, and with that, a decrease in additional cells being affected. 
Adverse effects are rare 
bronchospasm, dyspnoea and allergic reactions. 
Probably most effective as a prophylactic. May not be effective in reducing hospitalizations but it needs to be taken soon after the onset of infection.

## ppt/slides/slide76.xml
Normally a combination of at least two different drug classes
Reverse transcriptase inhibitors, protease inhibitors, fusion inhibitors, entry inhibitors and integrase inhibitors
Better longevity, less risk of infection to a partner
Needs to be started ASAP for best results
Needs to be taken every day
The ‘best medication is always changing due to new therapies that are more efficacious and better tolerated AND because the virus mutates
Example of fixed dose combination drugs: 
Triumeq
 which contains 
Dolu
tegravir
 (an Integrase inhibitor)

vudine
 
(Reverse-transcriptase inhibitor)

antiretroviral therapy (ART)

Platelets

## ppt/slides/slide97.xml
Glucan synthesis inhibitors 
Mechanism of action 
Caspo
fungin
 is an intravenously administered semisynthetic lipopeptide that inhibits the synthesis of glucan, a polysaccharide, in the cell walls of fungi. 
Caspo
fungin
 is effective against both Aspergillus (Farmers lung; aspergillosis) and Candida infections produced by strains not susceptible to other antimycotic drugs.
Common adverse effects
Bronchospasm and anaphylaxis, thrombophlebitis, headache and hypokalaemia 
Monitor liver function

## ppt/slides/slide98.xml
Antimetabolites
Mechanism of action
Flucytosine
 
is converted in fungal cells into its active form, 5-fluorouracil, and incorporated in RNA, making it defective
. Mammalian cells cannot activate 
flucytosine
 making this specific to fungi. 
The medicine is 
fungi
static
 
and is usually given by 
intravenous infusion
Common adverse effects 
As it is specific to fungi, adverse effects are few. But care must be taken in people with renal insufficiency, as blood levels may reach toxic levels due to an increase in half-life. 

## ppt/slides/slide99.xml
Miscellaneous agents: Griseofulvin
Griseofulvin
ORAL medication
Prevents fungal infection of hair/nails from spreading
Binds to the microtubules of mitotic fungal cells
Well tolerated but pregnancy category D

## ppt/slides/slide100.xml


## ppt/slides/slide101.xml
Non-azole sterol biosynthesis inhibitors
Mechanism of action
Terbinafine and butenafine disrupt ergosterol biosynthesis, leading to an inhibition of fungal cell wall synthesis. 
Oral treatment can cure onychomycosis (fungal infection of the nails), in as little as 12 weeks. 
Common adverse effects
Liver problems can occur with terbinafine, and gastrointestinal upsets occur in about 10 per cent of treated people.

[9.00] While we did say that the ideal antibiotic would not harm host cells, there are going to be some adverse effects of antibiotic therapy.
[20.00] And one of the biggest problems with antibiotic therapy is that it disrupts the natural flora on the skin, the gastrointestinal tract, and the vagina.
[32.88] The natural flora consists of a combination of very common bacteria as well as fungi.
[39.52] The toxins from the bacteria are going to keep the fungi in check,
[43.60] and the toxins from the fungi are going to keep the bacteria in check, and they just all live in harmony.
[50.64] And one of the major side effects of antibiotic therapy is that when we kill off a lot of the good bacteria,
[59.28] that leads to a proliferation of some of the really nasty bacteria and also some fungi as well.
[67.47] In the worst case scenario, that could lead to the client developing what we call a super infection.
[74.27] A super infection is a proliferation of a pathogenic microorganism to the point where it becomes an additional infection.
[84.03] Some bacterial super infections are very resistant to antibiotic therapy, as you'd imagine,
[90.55] because they occurred during the time that the person is on antibiotic therapy.
[98.11] The disruption of the flora in the JIT comes along with the most common side effect of the antibacterial therapy,

[319.40] Now, moving on to antibiotic resistance,
[323.36] there's a number of different factors that are related to antibiotic resistance, and those are identified here.
[331.44] One thing to keep in mind is that the bacteria are replicating at an incredible rate.
[337.64] And all along, some of those are developing spontaneous mutations, especially if they're being subjected to something like an antibiotic.
[349.12] And some of those mutations, those spontaneous mutations may result in something that protects them from the antibiotic.
[358.16] And then when that bacteria actually proliferates, all of its prodigy are going to develop that same mutation.
[370.91] So basically all of the prodigy from that bacteria have developed a mutation that makes them resistant to the antibiotic.
[382.63] So if you take a look at some of those factors that are related to antibiotic resistance, for instance,
[389.39] under dosage or poor compliance, if a client stops an antibiotic therapy before they've recovered from the infection,
[399.43] all of those microorganisms have been exposed to the antibiotic and the ones that are just starting to
[406.75] make some molecular changes that might be able to protect themselves from that particular antibiotic.
[413.83] They'll continue to make those molecular changes.
[417.15] And then, of course, like I said before, the prodigy of the the microorganism is going to be resistant to that antibiotic.
[430.10] Here's a list of things that we can do in order to make sure that we're not contributing to antibiotic resistance.
[437.66] I'll make sure that it's posted somewhere else in the module as well.
[442.66] One of the things that can be done, possibly not you, but you can encourage your colleagues to make sure that this is done,
[449.62] is to prescribe an antibiotic that the bacteria is proven to be sensitive to.
[456.74] And that involves a culture and sensitivity.
[461.34] For instance, if a client has a urinary tract infection, a broad spectrum antibiotic might be initiated in the very first place.
[470.70] But then after the culture and sensitivity results come in, that client should be changed over to the most effective antibiotic as soon as possible.
[481.26] And finally, for this portion of the module, we see an artist's depiction of a bacterium with the different Mechanisms that are antibiotics target.
[491.61] And as we said before,
[493.73] what we're going to be trying to do with our antibacterial agents is to find the differences in the bacterial cell as compared to the host cell.
[504.85] And for bacteria, it's not too difficult because bacteria are prokaryotes, whereas humans are eukaryotes.
[515.57] A prokaryote is a one celled organism. It lacks a membrane bound nucleus.
[521.97] And the other thing is that the bacteria have a cell wall.
[526.77] Not all bacteria, but most of them have a cell wall. And that, of course, is something that humans don't have.
[533.25] We have cell membranes and for instance, the penicillins and cephalosporins.
[538.81] They inhibit the cell wall synthesis. And then there's other antibiotics that destabilise the cell wall.
[546.97] Disrupting the cell wall in a bacteria is not compatible with life for that bacteria.
[554.08] As we said before, when we're talking about antifungal agents, we'll find that there's a lot more toxicity associated with that antifungal agent,
[565.88] because there's a lot more similarities between the eukaryotic cells of the human cell
[571.72] and the eukaryotic fungal cells than there are between prokaryotes and eukaryotes.

In many cases, a physician will treat a bacterial infection by giving a broad-spectrum antibiotic that has been proven to be successful in that general type of infection. However, if there is a good possibility that the infection is being caused by resistant bacteria, a Microscopy, Culture and Sensitivity (MC&S) should be performed in a lab, and a narrow spectrum antibiotic may be prescribed based on the results of the MC&S. Untitled Multiple Choice

true

Normally, the natural flora on the skin and GIT contains bacteria that release toxins which keep the fungi in check, and fungi that release toxins which keep the bacteria in check. Candida albicans infection (candidiasis), which is a fungal infection of the mouth, GIT and or vagina can occur due to antibiotic use because the antibiotics kill off many of the protective bacteria within the bacterial flora.

===== B/2: Specific antibiotics [_7758315_1] =====

B/2: Specific antibiotics

===== B/2: Specific antibiotics [_7758515_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758315_1?view=content&state=view
Specific Antibiotics 1
Our antibiotics can be grouped in many different ways. For instance, one grouping has them as time dependant versus dose dependent. But one of the most useful ways to group them is by mechanism of action. While we will not need to know the mechanism by which various types of antibiotics work, it is a convenient grouping that ultimately makes it easier to get a complete picture of each type of antibiotic. In this first lesson about specific antibiotics, we will look at the antibiotics that inhibit cell wall synthesis. This happens as the bacteria are reproducing, and the prodigy will be unable to make the cell wall needed to survive. Antibiotics that inhibit the cell wall synthesis include the penicillins (suffix: cillin) and the cephalosporins (prefix: cef or ceph). These contain a β (beta)-lactam ring that is the active portion of the drug. Bacteria often develop resistance to β-lactam antibiotics by synthesizing a β-lactamase, an enzyme that attacks the β-lactam ring before it can adversely affect the cell wall. To overcome this resistance, β-lactam antibiotics can be given with β-lactamase inhibitors such as clavulanic acid.

===== C/3: Antifungal agents [_7758317_1] =====

C/3: Antifungal agents

===== C/3: Antifungal agents [_7758517_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758317_1?view=content&state=view
A brief look at antifungal agents
Fungi are nature's way of degrading things, so if you want your belongings to last longer, make sure that you clean off the mould and mildew!
Fungi are found all over, and we have an astounding number of fungi that live in the natural skin flora, but the bacterial toxins from the resident bacteria keep the fungi in check. When an infection does occur from fungi, the term is a mycosis. That is where our antifungal infections come in, and unfortunately, because of the fact that fungal cells are so close to human cells, there is potential toxicity with most antifungal agents. The main difference between human cells and the fungal cells is that human cells contain cholesterol in the cell membrane, while fungal cell membranes contain ergosterol. So the ergosterol is the target of most of our antifungal agents.
Video: Fungi and antifungal agents
PHAR2001_6_3_1_Antifungal agents

Since fungal cells and human cells are both eukaryotic cells, it is difficult to treat systemic fungal infections without resulting in high levels of toxicity to the host. The client will need to drink plenty of water, and you will need to spell out the reason for this saying that if they do not, they will encounter liver and kidney toxicity. In addition, there are other measures we can take. Complete the best practice instructions to the client taking systemic antifungal agents with this drag and drop excercise.

===== D/4: VirusesViruses 1 [_7758518_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758318_1?view=content&state=view
Viruses 1
We knew very little about viruses until the HIV scare in the early 1980s but since then our understanding of viruses has been advancing comparatively quickly. As such, the first-line therapies for viral infections will change before you get to clinic. So the good news is that you do not need to know many antiviral medications at all. But you really do need to have a general knowledge of viruses and of the antiviral medications. The next two lessons attempt to give you that broad understanding.
Video: Antiviral medications
PHAR2001_6_4_1_Viruses_1
Activity: Test your knowledge

Links:
https://scu.au.panopto.com/Panopto/LTI/LTI.aspx

--- h5p_b560c0ee66560d4b1abd ---
false

Finish ?

Retry ?

Many viruses ‘hide’ in different portions of the body so that they are safe from immune surveillance. An example of this is the human immunodeficiency virus which hides in the dorsal root ganglion (where the cell bodies of the sensory nerves are)

Untitled True/False Question

true

One of the most important recommendations about antiviral medications is that they need to be administered almost as soon as any symptoms are felt. The reason for this is the exponential replication of the virus. Nearly every time viral particles are released from host cells, the host cell dies, leading to many of the symptoms. By the time we notice symptoms, there is mass destruction of cells already.

[545.86] a large percentage of the individuals who contact the hepatitis B and C develop what we call a chronic hepatitis B or chronic hepatitis C.

[571.92] after, you know, the constant, insidious damage,
[576.56] the liver starts developing fibrosis and scarring as the body tries to scar over that area in order to protect the liver.
[586.76] Remember what we always say chronic inflammation almost always results in fibrosis and scarring.
[594.84] So if there's anything that you can do to prevent that inflammation or decrease that inflammation, it should be done.
[603.08] The newest anti hepatitis viral therapies are direct acting antiviral agents, which are more similar to the therapy for human immunodeficiency virus.
[615.08] They target specific mechanisms of the hepatitis C and B,
[620.19] and most often the medications for hepatitis C are not the best choice for hepatitis B and vice versa.

[645.99] While the hepatitis B virus is usually just kept in check.

[681.54] And that's really important to tell the client because previously therapy was not very effective and was 6 to 12 months of injections with interferon,

[701.14] They were really, really difficult to take.

[719.58] And that is regardless of whether they are injecting drugs or not.
[726.10] Unfortunately, none of the direct acting antiviral agents for chronic hepatitis are suitable for pregnant women.
[734.54] Okay. Well, that concludes the final portion of the anti-infective pharmacology.

===== E/5: Anticancer chemotherapy [_7758520_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758320_1?view=content&state=view
Anticancer Chemotherapy
Normal cells are tightly regulated, and this ensures that they do not start proliferating out of control. When a group of cells lose these controls, they may become cancerous. In this lesson, we are going to take a general look at the types of anticancer chemotherapeutic agents and develop an understanding of why some of the common side effects from these agents develop.
Video: Anticancer medications
PHAR2001_6_5_1_Anticancer_Chemotherapy
Activity: Test your knowledge
undefined
Thanks for your participation!
Thanks for engaging in the activities associated with the medications that deliver selective toxicity. Our final topic is just a reflective piece that we really hope you enjoy.
Links:
https://scu.au.panopto.com/Panopto/LTI/LTI.aspx
undefined

--- h5p_d24ec58d896133b1feca ---
The chemotherapy agent stops the proliferation of red blood cells, leading to anaemia, and as such the client should be instructed on how to rise from a seated position.

The chemotherapy agent stops the proliferation of white blood cells, leading to decreased immunity and as such the client should be instructed to avoid large gatherings, avoid contact with animal manure, and take precautions that help prevent their exposure to bacteria.

The chemotherapy agent stops the proliferation of platelets, leading to increased bleeding, and as such, rectal thermometers should not be used, stool softeners should be used, and electric razors should be used in place of hand held razors.

The chemotherapy agent stops the proliferation of epithelial cells which leads to weak skin and easy bruising, so it should be recommended the person takes extreme care with sharp objects such as knifes.

The chemotherapy agent stops the proliferation of the epithelial skin lining the entire GIT leading to anorexia nervosa. The person should be instructed to eat as much of their favourite foods as possible.

Finish ?

Retry ?

Most chemotherapy drugs stop rapidly proliferating cells that are going through the cell cycle. This leads to the cancer cells being unable to replicate. But it also leads to other rapidly proliferating cells being unable to replicate the way they normally do, which in turn leads to some of the side effects of the medications. Which of the following statement(s) is/are correct?

[70.07] So what is cancer? Cancer is basically a set of cells that have started to undergo unregulated cell growth.
[80.59] Normally, healthy cells are programmed to die, but cancer cells don't die.
[87.03] They keep going through this cell cycle, increasing the number of cancer cells and crowding out other normal cells.
[97.55] We call this cell cycle, where you have different phases of the cell cycle, and the result is a new cell.
[105.39] Well, one of the most important things to understand about cancer cells is that they start going through that cell cycle over and over.
[115.31] The cells are no longer regulated in the way that normal cells are regulated.
[121.87] so. The most common way in which an anti-cancer drug is going to work is going to stop the cells from going through those phases of the cell cycle.
[134.07] If we can do that, the growth of the tumour is arrested.
[137.63] And as long as the tumour cells don't mutate in a way that makes them no longer susceptible to the therapy, the cancer cell is going to be stopped.
[147.51] But the bad news is that other cells are going through the cell cycle at the same time as the cancer cells are.
[156.23] And because those cells are no longer able to go through the cell cycle,
[161.79] we wind up with the most important and most common side effects of cancer therapy.
[169.55] So right now think about which cells will be going through that cell cycle over and over.

[232.54] Imagine what that's going to feel like if that regeneration of cells in the intestine is not allowed to occur.

[262.85] And therefore many nutritionists suggest that the client doesn't eat his or her favourite foods,
[270.25] because that nausea may give the person a lasting distaste for that food long after the treatment has ceased.

[285.37] There's hair loss, but the most significant area of concern is the bone marrow.
[291.17] Remember, that's where we make our red blood cells, our white blood cells and our platelets.
